2026 Ohio Governor’s Race: Full Interview with Vivek Ramaswamy

WSYX ABC 6(203.0K)·28:08·2026-10-26
Mike McCarthyAnchor, WSYX ABC 6 Vivek RamaswamyRepublican gubernatorial candidate, Ohio Amy ActonSubject of campaign ad
TL;DRRepublican gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy outlines his 2026 platform for Ohio, emphasizing immediate property tax rollbacks, stringent regulations on data centers, Medicaid fraud reduction, educational choice expansion, and economic growth through business experience and federal alignment.
Immediate Property Tax Relief: Goal: Implement the largest property tax rollback in Ohio history by resetting property taxes to pre-pandemic levels and capping future growth. Implementation Timeline: Target for homeowners to feel net financial impact by this time in 2027....

TL;DR

Republican gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy outlines his 2026 platform for Ohio, emphasizing immediate property tax rollbacks, stringent regulations on data centers, Medicaid fraud reduction, educational choice expansion, and economic growth through business experience and federal alignment.

Property & Income Taxes

  • Immediate Property Tax Relief:
    • Goal: Implement the largest property tax rollback in Ohio history by resetting property taxes to pre-pandemic levels and capping future growth.
    • Implementation Timeline: Target for homeowners to feel net financial impact by this time in 2027.
    • Funding Mechanism: Backstop local shortfalls (police, fire, schools, veteran services) using state economic growth surpluses, similar to the $1.6 billion gas tax suspension playbook.
  • Long-Term Income Tax Phasing:
    • Timeline: An 8- to 10-year trajectory to reach zero income tax to keep residents and investment capital from moving to zero-tax states like Florida and Tennessee.
    • Initial Steps (2027): Eliminate the capital gains tax to attract high-paying investment dollars without risking major immediate state revenue losses.
  • State Budget Context: Noted Ohio's $30 billion general revenue fund discretionary portion includes $8 billion for Medicaid, $12 billion for K-12 education, $3 billion for universities, and $3 billion for prisons.

Data Center Regulations & Economic Development

  • Strict Construction Conditions:
    • Mandates: Data centers must provide free electricity to local neighborhoods, eliminate property tax abatements, ensure discharged water is as clean as intake water, provide transparency (no NDAs), use vegetative insulation (trees), and build on abandoned industrial sites rather than farmland or residential areas.
    • Enforcement: Proposed an executive order to halt new data center construction and announcements until enabling legislation passes.
  • Tax Exemption Stance: Criticized sales and property tax exemptions for data centers as crony capitalism, emphasizing that abatements for data centers are "unforgivable" while homeowners face skyrocketing property taxes.
  • Alternative Growth: Prioritizing manufacturing booms and defense industrial bases in Springfield, Dayton, and Youngstown over building data centers without local community benefits.

Government Efficiency & Medicaid Reform

  • Waste, Fraud, and Abuse:
    • Medicaid Spending: Ohio spends $47 billion annually on Medicaid.
    • Fraud Estimates: Independent estimates suggest up to $4.7 billion annually is lost to shell companies, non-existent nursing homes, and fraudulent home healthcare providers.
    • Action Plan: Partner with the Attorney General starting in January to prosecute fraud and negotiate with the federal government to keep recovered savings in Ohio rather than sending them back to Washington D.C.

School Funding & School Choice

  • Educational Choice & Mobility:
    • Vouchers & District Choice: Supports continuing Ed-choice vouchers and expanding choice within public school districts (magnet programs, special needs) and across public districts.
    • Personal Background: Attended public schools for nine years before transferring to St. Xavier High School for high school.
  • Public School Improvements:
    • Fair School Funding Formula: Committed to keeping the current fair school funding formula as law.
    • Teacher Compensation: Wants to raise starting salaries for public school teachers above the current state average of approximately $38,000 per year.
    • Classroom Focus: Direct a greater portion of public education dollars away from non-classroom expenditures and toward direct classroom instruction.
    • Literacy Goal: Target 100% end-of-third-grade literacy for every medically capable student.

Campaign, Politics & Federal Relations

  • Relationship with Donald Trump: Emphasizes that having a governor with a strong relationship with the U.S. President is an advantage for securing federal investment dollars from the $900 billion flowing into the U.S., helping Ohio punch above its weight compared to states like Texas, Florida, California, and New York.
  • Criticism of Opponent: Criticized the Democratic opponent (former Health Director Amy Acton) for quitting her prior health director post, refusing to participate in debates (such as those offered by CNN and NBC), refusing cameras at events, and blockading interactions at a Fraternal Order of Police barbecue.
  • Personal Motivations: Highlighted his background as a middle-class kid from Evendale, Ohio, whose father worked at the GE plant and mother worked in nursing homes and the VA, and underscored his success building Roivant, a $25–$30 billion NASDAQ-listed biotech company that developed 6 FDA-approved medicines.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Property Tax Relief Timeline: Ramaswamy predicts direct property tax relief will be felt by homeowners by this time in 2027.
  • Income Tax Elimination Timeline: Ramaswamy states that reaching zero state income tax will follow an 8- to 10-year trajectory.
  • Capital Gains Elimination: Ramaswamy predicts the state will eliminate capital gains taxes in 2027 to attract investment dollars.
  • Literacy Benchmark: Ramaswamy sets a target to eventually achieve 100% end-of-third-grade literacy for every medically capable student in Ohio.

Notable Quotes

"If you've lived in a home for 20 or 30 years, you bought it, you owned it outright, that is your home, your castle, your property. But the fact that you have to move out because the government has hiked your taxes, that's not right and I have to fix it." — Vivek Ramaswamy
"If when you and I sit down a year from now we're talking about property taxes or data centers, that means I will have failed as Governor and I don't intend to fail." — Vivek Ramaswamy
"Education begins in the household. That means also bringing some parental accountability here for chronic absenteeism. These are problems that we have to fix in education that go beyond just budgetary problems." — Vivek Ramaswamy

And that's the spirit of ambition and boldness that I want to bring to Ohio. First of all, thank you for making time for us. And that's the most important thing to me and it is something that I will continue to protect and to continue to do when I'm Governor of this state. She's the true hero of the family and I'm so grateful to our family and particularly Apoorva for really making it possible to serve in this way. You've pledged to slash property taxes immediately, calling it the largest property tax rollback in Ohio history. How are you going to make that happen and how soon realistically would people feel any sort of real benefit or net impact at their homes? So I'm hoping that people feel net impact by this time next year. By this time in 2027, I'm hopeful that we will be able to give direct property tax relief to homeowners and we want to get that done. The real issue here is that it's after the pandemic that property taxes went totally crazy in the state. And I've met seniors across Ohio in different parts of our state, many of whom feel they have to move out of their home to be able to afford life because they can't make the property taxes work. That doesn't make any sense. If you've lived in a home for 20 or 30 years, you bought it, you owned it outright, that is your home, your castle, your property. But the fact that you have to move out because the government has hiked your taxes, that's not right and I have to fix it. Now if this is a post-pandemic problem our solution is really simple. Let's reset the property taxes to where they were pre-pandemic before the end of the pandemic and then cap the growth from there. And it turns out we can do that without hurting local police, fire, schools, or veteran services. That's practical. I haven't said eliminate property taxes, I've said I'm going to do that. And I know I can get that done and the way we're able to do it is when necessary, use some of our state's surpluses to backstop any shortfalls in local counties or local schools or local police or fire that need it. And it's a great example of how we use those surpluses was in recent weeks when we got the gas tax suspended. I worked with our legislature, we got into the details and we used $1.6 billion in surpluses that our state has from economic growth to be able to provide people gas tax relief. It's going to be the same playbook that I use to give people property tax relief in a big way next year. When it comes to the income tax, you've talked about phasing that out as well. Yeah. Priority number one when you get into office is it getting that done? Priority number one is the property taxes. That is urgency, that's really what's important. The income tax is important in the long run because that's what brings more dollars into Ohio. It turns out we have a lot of people, many people watching this program might know some of them, who spend six months and a day in a place like Florida or Tennessee or zero tax state. Ohio is losing money because of that. Those are Ohioans who would rather live here, but are doing it for tax reasons. Then there's investment that doesn't come to this state, but goes to places like Texas or Tennessee or other states, or Florida. I want those investment dollars flowing into Ohio because that benefits Ohioans. So we have a clear plan to get there tied to economic growth. The good news is, to give credit to the prior administration, they've brought the income tax rate down from nearly 5% where it was at the start of the pandemic to now nearly, it's about 2.7%. That's a good bit of progress and one of the things we learned is that state revenue actually exceeded our expectations when you brought the rate down because more people are staying in the state, more money is flowing into the state. So I want to continue that, but that's a longer term objective, my nearest term deliverable when I take office is we're going to crush those property taxes and do it in a way that's practical and that's what homeowners are going to get when I take office. So would you at all start looking at rolling back the income tax, phasing it out once you take office if elected? We're going to take some initial steps in that direction in 2027, one of those that I've talked about is looking at, for example, capital gains tax elimination. That's one where we don't even get that much revenue from that as a state, but that helps attract investment dollars into Ohio, bringing high-paying jobs to families. So that's a benefit that I think we can deliver on in 2027. But to get to zero income tax that's going to be an 8 to 10-year trajectory to get there. Property taxes are going to be immediate next year. That's not the way our politics is supposed to work and so I will make a pledge that I will fight for every one of the families in the state whether they vote for me or not, I will fight for them equally to have a better life, but it does require the people who want lower property taxes and lower electric bills and to crush crime in our cities, and to do so aggressively while supporting law enforcement. If you agree on those things, I'll ask you to get out and vote and respectfully to vote for me. And if you do your part, I'm going to do mine in bringing this state together and putting more money in your pockets, and securing safer streets for our families. good things are going to happen. And that's the spirit of ambition and boldness that I want to bring to Ohio. We were that state, we led this country when we led the first industrial revolution, we were the state of Neil Armstrong and John Glenn and the Wright Brothers and Thomas Edison. And in fact, more U.S. presidents born and raised in Ohio to this day than any other state in the country. I think we can be that state again if we have a governor who believes in being bold, in fighting for the people of this state. That's exactly what I'm going to do, Apoorva and I are in this together and we're going to fight every day for the people of this state to have a better life, and that is something that I've learned over the course of my career, both as a businessman and as the husband of a now successful surgeon, we've learned that's what it takes. Anything else? We're tight on time. Yeah. I'll tell you why I'm running. I've interviewed a lot of people for jobs in my career, probably thousands of people. And one of the things I love to know about somebody is their motivation for why they want a job. Well, now I'm on the other side of the table, right? This entire campaign is really like a job interview. I'm interviewing for this process, and I report to you, the people of this state. So, I think I owe it to you to tell you why I'm doing this. I am doing this because I am grateful to this state and to this country for giving a kid like me, opportunities that would not have been available to my family in any other country on planet Earth. I grew up middle class. My dad worked at the GE plant off I-75 in Evendale, Ohio. My mom worked in nursing homes taking care of patients, worked at the VA. My dad was on a five-figure salary. I went to public schools for the first nine years of my life. They didn't have childcare. So, when my dad had to go to night school for four years, I went with him, including when I was in sixth grade for a 45-minute drive each way to Northern Kentucky University Law School. I saw how they dealt with hardship. It was through hard work and through teaching us those values. But now we're raising our kids in circumstances here in Columbus that my family and Apoorva's family would not have dreamed of just 30 or 40 years ago. That's the story of the American dream, and I believe it exists because I've lived it. But my faith also teaches me that we have a moral duty to use the blessings that we've been given to leave this place better than we found it. To leave those around us even more blessed because of it. And rather than doing it through philanthropy or other ways, which we've done some of, I actually want to do it in the way that I believe I can have the biggest impact. And that's as governor of this state. And I want to use those skills and the opportunities that Ohio has given to me and to my family to pass that same American dream on to the next generation of Ohioans and really to turn that American dream into an American reality for our kids in their generation. That's what motivates me. And I think we can do it. And if I win this election, I consider it my moral mission and my moral duty to actually see that through. That's why I'm in it. And with the help of voters across the state, we're going to get it done. Thank you very much.

AI Crisis in Math: Navier-Stokes, Riemann Hypothesis & Complexity Theory | Lex Fridman Podcast #503

Lex Fridman(5.1M)·214:27·697.5K views·2026-10-26
Lex FridmanHost, Lex Fridman Podcast Ankur MoitraMathematician, MIT Steven BruntonMathematician, UW
TL;DRRecent AI breakthroughs solving monumental mathematical problems—including Navier-Stokes, the quasi-Riemann hypothesis, the unique games conjecture, and Fermat's Last Theorem—have sparked a crisis of opportunity and existential uncertainty among mathematicians regarding the future of human research, education, and institutional playbooks.
The OpenAI and Anthropic Drops: - Over a period of weeks, AI systems claimed solutions to centuries-old problems including Navier-Stokes, the quasi-Riemann hypothesis, the unique games conjecture, and hundreds of other results. - Anthropic produced a 13-million-line...

TL;DR

Recent AI breakthroughs solving monumental mathematical problems—including Navier-Stokes, the quasi-Riemann hypothesis, the unique games conjecture, and Fermat's Last Theorem—have sparked a crisis of opportunity and existential uncertainty among mathematicians regarding the future of human research, education, and institutional playbooks.

The State of Mathematical AI & Recent Breakthroughs

  • The OpenAI and Anthropic Drops:
    • Over a period of weeks, AI systems claimed solutions to centuries-old problems including Navier-Stokes, the quasi-Riemann hypothesis, the unique games conjecture, and hundreds of other results.
    • Anthropic produced a 13-million-line sorry-free formal Lean proof of Fermat's Last Theorem developed with minimal human intervention (primarily gentle prompting like "Do a breakthrough").
    • Ankur Moitra noted that while rumors had swirled for weeks regarding RL=L, unique games, and matrix multiplication, the actual drop left him feeling intellectually lonely and forced a realization that the traditional academic playbook is thrown out the window.
  • The Solved Axiom Discussion:
    • Alex Kontorovich introduced the "solved axiom": assuming any mathematical problem solvable through human-AI interaction could also be solved by AI alone.
    • Kontorovich does not personally believe the solved axiom, arguing that current AI solutions often interpolate human knowledge (the "semigroup" model of chaining known moves) rather than pioneering genuinely new conceptual frameworks, though breadth and opaqueness-reduction are unprecedented.
  • Verification and Autoformalization:
    • Autoformalization is effectively solved for translating known proofs into verified software assistants like Lean and Mathlib.
    • Mathlib operates similarly to a GitHub pull request model where maintainers and automated verifiers check logical steps down to the Peano axioms, ensuring 100% correctness without natural language ambiguity.

Navier-Stokes & Fluid Dynamics

  • Mathematical Significance:
    • Steven Brunton explained that Navier-Stokes equations represent conservation of momentum for fluids, featuring a quadratic nonlinearity (similar to $X' = X^2$) that can lead to finite-time blowup if uninhibited.
    • The recent AI breakthrough constructed a highly specialized, non-physical smooth external forcing scenario in 3D that produces infinite vorticity (blowup), answering a fundamental existence-and-uniqueness question.
  • Applied Engineering Reality:
    • Brunton emphasized that while the million-dollar math problem was cracked, day-to-day engineering fluids remain unchanged; predicting turbulence and achieving efficient inverse design for airplanes, wind turbines, and artificial hearts remains a trillion-dollar problem governed by multi-scale complexity.

Number Theory & Complexity Theory

  • Riemann Hypothesis & Primes:
    • Alex Kontorovich detailed the Riemann hypothesis, where the Riemann zeta function acts as a signal-processing tool recovering the distribution of prime numbers (the multiplicative building blocks of whole numbers) through the zeros of the zeta function.
    • The AI-proven quasi-Riemann hypothesis established a zero-free vertical strip with real part between $7/8$ and $1$, a major advance beyond a 70-year-old boundary, though reaching $1/2$ (full RH) requires fundamentally new ideas.
  • Unique Games & Complexity:
    • Ankur Moitra discussed the Unique Games Conjecture, which strengthens the PCP theorem and provides optimal hardness-of-approximation for constraint satisfaction problems.
    • Despite human strides (such as Dor Minzer and Irit Dinur's 2-to-1 games theorem), the AI's positive proof of the Unique Games Conjecture confirmed a strong organizing hypothesis that surprised complexity theorists.
  • P vs. NP and Beyond Worst-Case Analysis:
    • Moitra considers $P \neq NP$ the hardest problem unlikely to fall soon.
    • He advocates for "beyond worst-case analysis," noting that real-world natural data and algorithms (like SAT solvers and LLMs) routinely bypass worst-case computational hardness because natural systems and language have evolved to make problems tractable.

The Human Element: Metaphors, Motivation, and Education

  • The Doorman Fallacy:
    • Moitra highlighted the "doorman fallacy"—the mistake of reducing a complex job (like a mathematician or a doorman) to its most visible mechanical task (proving theorems or opening a door), ignoring the broader value of building theories, making conceptual connections, signaling brand value, mentoring, and asking the right questions.
  • The Shift in Academic Playbooks:
    • Terence Tao and other mathematicians expressed horror that slow, deliberate PhD research paths are being disrupted by instant AI solutions.
    • Graduate students face anxiety over 3-to-6-month horizons rather than long-term problem-solving.
    • The panelists advise students to maintain curiosity, practice critical reasoning ("advisor skills" to evaluate and push back on AI outputs), and pretend they already have tenure to pursue intrinsically motivated questions.

Notable Quotes

"There's a lot of opportunity in that crisis. It's a chance to rethink what it is we're trying to accomplish... after millennia of having a candle that we are walking around the woods... Now we have a flashlight." — Alex Kontorovich
"The playbook and the blueprint for success that we had for so long is now thrown out the window... it feels like we've lost some of that patience in terms of planting the seeds from which the great oak trees grow." — Ankur Moitra
"We solved the million-dollar math problem. There's a trillion-dollar engineering problem, which is, like, how do you predict these things in a fast, efficient way and do this inverse design?" — Steven Brunton
"Math is not the fact, it's the why. It's always been the why. It's our deepest quest for understanding. And if you just tell me a fact is true, but I don't understand why it's true, that has contributed zero to my knowledge." — Alex Kontorovich

As a quick aside, can you mention what Mathlib is and Lean, just 'cause we mentioned it a few times for folks? Sure. So mathematics is, in the ideal, a formal process starting with an axiomatized system and then logical rules of inference from there. So if people think of -- I don't know, do people still learn Euclid's geometry in high school or something? That's supposed to be the kind of model for kids to start seeing proofs for the first time and seeing an axiomatic structure. And you can take that idea and write it in software and say, "Here are the actual axioms of mathematics. Here are the rules of logical inference." And Lean, there's a long history of this kind of software. It's called interactive theorem provers, where you're writing line by line in a proof, and the computer says, "Yes, this line does follow by logical inference from the goal state on the previous line." So Lean is software that allows you to check all kinds of things. It can check the specifications of your code align with what you, what you are trying to define. That's what it was -- what it was invented for was bug-free code. And hopefully, in the future, we will have bug-free code because AI systems are really good at finding bugs in code written by humans, so that's-- it's becoming ever more important for that application. But another application is to check mathematical results and allow you to write a mathematical result in a very formal way. And so now Lean itself is just the hardware that checks the logical steps. You do not want to start every math project by defining what the natural numbers are, using zero and the successor, you know, the Peano axioms of the natural numbers. So it's nice to have a large general library that's extremely well maintained, extremely well organized, and so on. And that's what Mathlib is. Mathlib is the Lean library for formalizing mathematics in the Lean ecosystem. And again, Lean is just one interactive theorem prover. There are lots of others. It's the one that so far has been winning the math wars, if there are any math wars. It's a competition of two people or something. And it's the technology used in part to show that the OpenAI release of all the different proofs, that the proofs are correct. That's right. It has become now standard to, because autoformalization has gotten to the point where AI systems can not only prove a theorem in natural language. You can't trust their natural language proofs. These are stochastic processes. And so the fact that they're saying things doesn't mean that they necessarily have a model of mathematics in their heads, whatever that means, the same way that we do. But that's okay 'cause they'll write a Lean -- Lean code that builds on top of Mathlib, builds on top of the Prime Number Theorem Pro-- Plus project or whatever, and checks that it has indeed solved this problem down to the axioms. So fundamentally, the Navier-Stokes equations is just a way of stating the conservation of momentum for fluids, for objects that can move kind of freely, like gases and liquids. So we know F equals ma is conservation of momentum, you know, classical Newtonian physics. The same basic principle applies to the gas in this room, to water flowing, and it's true in any volume. So it's true in little volumes, big volumes, kind of imaginary volumes. And if you write down that conservation of momentum in a volume, and then you kind of take the limit as that volume becomes arbitrary and small, you get these Navier-Stokes equations. It's a differential equation, a partial differential equation that describes how the velocity field of a fluid changes in space and time, and also the pressure related to the forces acting on that fluid. Can you give intuition about why fluid, given that F equals MA is a pretty simple equation, why fluids are such complicated systems? F equals MA is simple to write down, but it can get complicated really, really quickly. So if I have, like, a single pendulum, it's really easy to write down the equations for that single pendulum. If I put, you know, another pendulum on the end of it, now I have a more complicated equation, and it gives chaos. Fluids is, you know, in a sense, if I try to model the air in this room, I could chop the room up into a billion little boxes and write down a little differential equation for how the momentum works in all of those billion little boxes, but now I have a really complicated system of equations. And so it's simple to write down, it's simple to conceptually understand what the equation is saying and representing, but actually solving it and understanding how the fluid actually moves in any kind of practical sense is very, very complicated. I read something like, you know, like one space where fluid dynamics is important is reducing drag for when you're flying, for airplanes. So drag reduction on airplanes, better artificial hearts, you know, more efficient wind turbines and gas turbines and, you know, you name it. Sounds good. Cool. Ankur, one of my favorite topics is theoretical computer science. You're one of the world experts in many of its problems. So I'd love to ask you about one of the big problems that's been solved in this OpenAI drop, which is the unique games conjecture. Can you give people an intuition about what this problem is, why it's a big deal, and what are some aspects of the proof that are interesting? Yes, I'd love to. So let me give you a little bit of a background. So let's start off with complexity theory 101. So one of the most important problems in theoretical computer science is the satisfiability problem. So the thing to keep in your mind is imagine you've got variables X one to XN, and they can take on either true or false values. And what I'm gonna do is I'm gonna create a big formula out of them, and usually, the way we do this is with an and of ors. So I start off with a clause that's, you know, an or, X one or not X three or X seven. So depending on my assignment, that clause might be satisfied as long as at least one of those things is true. And then I take the and of many of these different clauses together. And the question is, if I give you that formula, called a conjunctive normal form formula, you know, can you find a satisfying assignment? So can you find an assignment of X one, X two, up to XN, setting them either to be equal to true or false, so that all of those or clauses are on, so that the entire formula evaluates to true? So this is the canonical example of a problem that's easy to check someone else's answer, but it might be hard to find an answer yourself. So if you told me that it's satisfiable, all I would do is I'd ask you, "Okay, give me the satisfying assignment." You tell me what X one is set to, what X two is set to, and I just go clause by clause and check to make sure that you really have satisfied everything. Now, the, you know, the central problem in theoretical computer science is the P versus NP problem. So are problems that are easy to check, is it easy to find their solutions to? And the presumption in our field is that no, it's not easy, and that's the basis you know, of a lot of things like the, you know, cryptography and e-commerce and what have you. Now, really, the starting point for getting to something like the unique games conjecture is something called the PCP theorem. So this was a landmark result proved in the nineties. It actually was such a big deal that it made the front page of The New York Times. And the crux of it is really this idea that it's even robustly hard to fool a verifier. So you start off with a formula, and either it's satisfiable or not satisfiable, right? And what does that mean? If it's satisfiable, you can write down an assignment, and I can check one by one that the entire thing evaluates to true. But if it's not satisfiable, that means someplace you're lying. You write down this purported satisfying assignment, and when I go clause by clause, I'm quickly gonna find one clause that is not true, and that invalidates the whole thing. And I say, "Aha, I caught you. It's not actually a satisfying assignment." So the idea behind things like probabilistically checkable proofs, without getting into a lot of the technical definitions, the idea is really gap amplification. So can you start off with a formula that is hard to decide whether it's satisfiable or not? And can you engineer a new, maybe much bigger formula that's robustly easy to check in the sense that if it's satisfiable, it'll still be satisfiable. You can write down an assignment, and it'll be fine. But if it's not, I should actually be able to just flip a few coins, query random variables, check a random clause, and with good probability find that you're lying. So the point is that with very sublinear inspection, with only constant amount of inspection, I can find that you're cheating. So this was a momentous result in theoretical computer science because it laid the foundation really for what's called hardness of approximation. So a lot of algorithms and complexity theory is what do you do when you're faced with problems that are genuinely hard to solve? And, you know, one of the things we do is we try to approximately solve them. So maybe we can't find an assignment if there is one that satisfies all the clauses, but can we find one that satisfies many of the clauses? How large a fraction of the clauses? Now, even there, we would expect problems to become computationally hard after a certain point. It should be that it's not just that, you know, deciding whether it's completely satisfiable or not completely satisfiable is hard, but maybe even deciding is it 100% satisfiable or is it only 99% satisfiable. The best case over all assignments satisfy at most 99% of the clauses. That too might end up being hard to decide. So these are what probabilistically checkable proofs give us. In places where we knew that the optimization problem was hard, they tell us that it's even hard to approximate. And the Unique Games Conjecture is a strengthening of the ordinary PCP theorem because it has to do with a certain type of label cover instance. It's a bit technical to go into the details, but the point is that if that version of a probabilistically checkable proof were true, it would have this great organizing effect where it would turn out that for a huge swath of problems, we would actually know what is the best approximation algorithm, and it would be something very simple. So when I started off in the field, the Unique Games Conjecture had been posed, you know, a few years before that, and there was still a lot of debate about whether it should be true or false. And, you know, people worked hard to try and give, you know, proofs of it. People worked hard to try and disprove it. There was this amazing time period, maybe in the 2010s, where there was quite a lot of back and forth and, you know, there would be new results came out that people's opinions would change whether they believed unique games or not. I like this quip. I forget who said it, so I can't properly attribute it, but someone told me, "You know, maybe God's gonna decide whether it's true or false based on which side is working harder." Because it really seemed to change year to year. And you know, one of the results, one of the headlining results in the OpenAI drop of proofs was that the Unique Games Conjecture is true. Now, I should mention that this is not without spectacular human progress on it. So one of my colleagues at MIT, one of my colleagues at IES, they both contributed, among other people, to a lot of the major strides. So Dor Minzer and Irit Dinur, they proved a weaker version called the Two-to-One Games Theorem, and this really was a, you know, shot heard round the world in theoretical computer science because at that point, I think a lot of people weren't sure what was a path forward for Unique Games Conjecture, and I think their work really gave a viable path forward to something that was in the vicinity of Unique Games. So it sounded like, you know, maybe humans could get there with another 10, 20 years. But, you know, just when OpenAI released the results, it turns out that the Unique Games Conjecture is actually true. So this is quite remarkable because you know, at some points I, I guess I did find myself on the side of the doubters because Unique Games just sounded too nice to be true because, you know a lot of work like Prasad Raghavendra's work, it implies optimal hardness of approximation for so many different types of constraint satisfaction problems that the conclusion just looked to me too strong to be true. What did you feel when you found out that night? Okay. Well, for starters, there were already rumors, you know, in the weeks leading up that OpenAI had solved the Unique Games Conjecture and that it was true. And these weren't even just completely arbitrary rumors. One of my colleagues, Dor Minzer, he actually posted a paper where he wrote something in the footnote at the bottom that, you know, "We would have liked to polish this paper more, but because of the rumors that were going around, we feel the need to put out our approach sooner rather than later." So for me, it was in some sense a bit of a relief that the results were finally out there. Now they're not very readable papers. It's not like you can point to them and really quickly identify what's a new key idea. But I'm glad that they're out there because now we can move forward on the next phase, which is digesting those proofs and figuring out where the field moves after that. Now, in terms of how did I feel that night you know, as Alex mentioned, we, you know, we all had a drink in hand. And then I saw, you know, on my phone the news that these proofs had dropped. And you know, people kept up coming and talking to me at the gala, but it's already hard to socialize as a mathematician... let alone, you know, when you have math you really want to read. So it was doubly hard. The other funny thing about it was, you know, I was in New York already, so I took it as an opportunity before the gala to visit a lot of people I knew at NYU. And I just happened to be meeting with Subhash Khot, the one who introduced the unique games conjecture. Are you gonna read it?" And he told me, "No." Which, you know, that's a lot of people's reactions, unfortunately. It's just kind of true that there are these grand challenges we work on, and sometimes when they get solved you know, sometimes it feels like, you know, someone has stolen your thunder. You know, it's not like people didn't have ideas about how to solve the unique games conjecture, and I bet you those blueprints would have panned out if we'd given them more time. I think the other thing I worry is that you know, that we lose out on the human effort of trying to reach those solutions to begin with. You know, math is not just about what is the furthest I can reach with the current set of tools I have. Is it a convex hull? Is it a subgroup? I don't know. But, you know, sometimes when it's a human-driven solution, we learn a lot more in the process. So I worry about the fact that there were these great landmark problems, field-defining problems that a lot of people were reaching towards, and that decisive progress was made, you know, and the results all dropped in one night, and that people lose their enthusiasm for going down those paths. And I wonder if we'd given those people that space to go down those paths, what other exciting things we would have discovered. You know, we wanted to understand the world, and mathematicians are very good at abstracting things. We should mention that the polynomial time or exponential time or super linear time, all this refers to worst case analysis. That's right. That's right. So can you explain what worst case analysis is, and what are its- ...Pitfalls. Yeah, I should say what are its pitfalls, 'cause I think it's a little bit counterintuitive from people outside the field, like to, like, to understand exactly when we say a certain problem is hard, you're actually talking about worst case. It's like analyzing, I don't know, I don't know why that popped into my head, but like marriage. It's like analyzing whether I should marry this person or not by saying, "Well, what's the worst thing that could possibly happen?" I think that's for human relationships, maybe it's not such a good strategy. Maybe the average case is a, is a better guide for making decisions. Anyway, I don't know why I brought that up. Okay, I have no advice about who you should marry. This is com-- yeah, complexity theory does not give you. I think there is some-- Isn't there a dating is it like an optimal stopping theory of the optimal- ...Secretary problem. The secretary problem. There's a lot of different names for it- ... but there is a mathematical way. There's an optimal strategy. So first, so what is worst case analysis? So- ...Yes, please ... you know, I was describing the SAT problem earlier, and the algorithmic problem you want to solve is, given any formula, you want to decide is it satisfiable or not. Now, what's crucial there is that when I'm asking to solve that algorithmic problem, I'm not telling you anything more about the structure of the formula. I'm not telling you, you know, these are the size of the clauses. They look like this. If I drew a graph connecting them, it would have this topology. So that's what worst case analysis is about, is it's about the premise that you can treat all of these computational problems actually by ignoring a lot of the fine-grained detail about the individual structure, and you can try and solve them, you know, for all possible inputs. And that's been spectacularly powerful because we've identified lots of algorithmic problems that really can be solved in the worst case and can be solved really efficiently. You know, like finding the max flow, as an example. But the question is, you know, whether or not that's a useful abstraction. So sometimes maybe you really do need to know more about the structure of the instance you're faced with in order to solve it better, right? And that's really an open question. This is the area of what's called beyond worst case analysis, and I would sort of argue that, you know, it's a very important direction for theoretical computer science to go in. It's hard to figure out what you should articulate about natural problems that is really the right level of detail that your algorithm can exploit it and can do better things than it can in the worst case. But let's use an example. So you were talking about the secretary problem. So the way that this works is it's an example of something called online algorithms. So you have a sequence of options coming. One through N. They come in each time step, and your goal is, you know, when someone comes along, you know their ranking relative to everyone else you've seen before, but you don't know their ranking relative to the people who are yet to come. And your goal is to, you know, to make a decision when the person comes along, do you commit or do they move on, in such a way that you have a good chance of finding the absolute best candidate. And so the point is that you can actually get a constant competitive ratio algorithm that what you do, the best strategy is to wait some amount of the time, not make any decisions, don't accept anyone. And then once that probationary window has passed, you take the first person who's better than anyone you've seen thus far. And that'll give you a good competitive ratio in the sense that you can prove theorems. Assuming the inputs arrive in a uniformly random order, you're likely to do pretty well compared to as if you knew what that sequence of inputs was in advance. So there's, you know, under online algorithms, there's this veil of uncertainty, which is, I don't know what the sequence of inputs are gonna look like. I wanna do well no matter what that sequence is because, you know, I don't really know how to model what that sequence looks like. Now, you can instead say, "Hey, maybe the algorithms I derive aren't good enough for my applications. Can I do better by trying to model more of the world around me?" And then there's kind of an art to figuring out what you should model, what you should build into your model, and what you shouldn't. And that seems to me like a tremendously important area, to explore. So for example, one other variant of, you know, online algorithms that's really important is the paging problem. So your laptop solves this problem all the time. You know, it's getting a sequence of requests of things that it wants to recall from memory. And the question is, what should it actually put in its cache, the part that's the easiest to access? And the problem again is that you don't know what that sequence of requests looks like in advance. So you can study the online algorithms problem of, you know, what is a good policy for managing the cache? You know, when something gets accessed, you know, I have to put it in, but what do I evict? And you can design algorithms that are good in terms of their competitive ratio. The number of times they miss in terms of those accesses is not much worse than, the best offline optimum if someone told me that sequence of requests in advance and I could optimize just for that sequence of requests. Now, this is a problem that's been studied since the '80s and '90s. The trouble is somehow that, you know, the best algorithms you can give, we know tight upper and lower bounds, they're just not that very good. You know, the number of misses they make compared to the best offline optimum is a large factor off from it, and that just doesn't compete with what your laptop is able to do right now because it really makes very few cache misses at the end of the day. So you're stuck with this problem which sort of bridges mathematical thinking with trying to model the natural world, which is, you know, what is it about realistic sequences of requests that makes them not the worst case? This is really an inverse problem now because it's, you know, the other side of the coin. Instead of someone handed you a problem that you're just trying to solve in the worst case, and we can all agree on how it's formulated, and we know what's a better algorithm, it does better on the competitive ratio, now you're stuck with this very fuzzy problem, which is: how should I model the world around me? And in a lot of ways, algorithmic thinking is necessary for figuring out what are productive ways to model that problem. Because it's not that you're gonna build a totally realistic model of what sequence of page requests look like, but how am I gonna coarsen the problem in just the right way that it leads to interesting algorithmic problems that teach me about the world, that force me to, you know, design new approaches to solving these very basic problems. So I'd argue that, you know, sort of coming out of this space where we are only solving well-defined problems, but we're actually using our mathematical and algorithmic insights to figure out where to dig in the first place is now more important than ever. It should have always been important, but you know, that to me is really gonna be thrust into the spotlight. Like a simple example is we were talking about factoring numbers. Well, factoring a random large number is really easy. Half of them are even. You know, factoring means find a factor. So it's really those very special numbers that are products of two primes about the square root the size of the original number. That's hard factoring, sorry. Which makes me think one of the things you worked on is mathematics of LLMs, AI systems, neural networks, and those things are pretty damn good at modeling the natural world. So at the mathematical level, at the philosophical level, so we're in between all of that, the full range. I don't know how we even enter that. Okay. So that's a big question. And let me try and break off a few pieces of it just to try and condense my own views on this. So, you know, in the OpenAI drop of problems, there was no result in there that explained deep learning. There was no result in there that explained LLMs. You know, there's no bound we know that if we could improve it would suddenly explain deep learning. The problems are just not posed that way. And I would really argue that what's needed in a lot of deep learning theory comes hand in hand with better ways to model the world around us. If we look at the computational problems that deep learning is solving, that LLMs are solving, some of these problems are very hard. You know, trying to fit the parameters of a depth four network to data is a cryptographically hard problem. We know that. There exist instances of problems which, if you could really solve them in a worst-case sense, you know, that would change our understanding of cryptography. And yet these problems get solved all the time, every day, when we're fitting deep -- you know, much deeper networks than that. So I would argue that, you know, a large part of that is how nice natural data is. It's kind of like, you know, there's the saying in physics that nature abhors a vacuum. I guess my version of it is that in some sense, nature abhors computationally hard problems. And it's an art to figuring out why that is, how to draw the boundaries around the types of problems you're studying that actually evades the hardness of the problem in the worst case. So there's an interesting example back from the '90s. So certainly back then, and we still are, very interested in protein folding. Now, you can model this in a worst-case sense. I've got a sequence of amino acids. I've got some energy function based on different potential 3D structures, and I want to find the 3D structure that minimizes how much energy there is. Now, this problem is computationally very hard, and yet nature solves these problems all the time. So why is that? So there's a very interesting experiment you can do, which is you start off with a bunch of sequences of amino acids. You try out some heuristics for folding them into 3D structures. These are things that don't solve the problem in the worst case. They just seem to do the right thing sometimes, for whatever that means. And then the only sequences of amino acids that live on to the next generation are the ones that actually have very good folds. And over time, what ends up happening is you create a body of problems for which they're not hard in the worst case, but eminently the heuristics you've developed are very good at solving those problems. So in a lot of ways, you know, what are the structure of proteins that nature seems to fold all the time? Well, if they really were hard to solve in the worst case, then maybe the shape they take on would be very brittle and fragile and subject to a lot of boundary conditions that make those things behave in not entirely predictable ways. So in some sense, you know, a lot of our data has evolved in ways to make these computational problems easier. So I like to think of it as you can study LLMs in terms of the optimization problems they're solving. You can study deep learning that way. And if you look at it from a worst-case perspective, you're gonna get very hard problems that you just tell people, "They're hard. Give up." But apparently, not giving up was a really good idea, and I think what makes those problems easy you cannot extract from the structure of natural data. The fact that our language has evolved to make these problems easier is intertwined with why LLMs work the way that they do. Great question. I mean, I think alignment is one of the most important topics we're facing right now, right? And, you know, just because it's very hard doesn't mean we don't make progress towards understanding it. So one of the things that I'd worked on recently, we were working on things called low-rank language models, which are a very simple abstraction of language models, and we were proving new algorithmic guarantees. But then it turned out that real language models really do have that kind of structure, at least in an approximate sense, and we were just pulling at this thread to see where this idea goes. And one of the really interesting things that it did was it shed some light on something called subliminal learning. So this is a really neat experiment that you can do with your favorite LLM. So you take two LLMs, LLM A and LLM B. For LLM A, you tell it a system prompt that says, "You really love owls. Every conversation topic, you try to bring up owls if it's at all germane." And then you ask it something concrete like generate random numbers. Now, there's no way to squeeze owls into that, so it'll generate random looking numbers, forty-eight, thirty-seven, eight, whatever. And you can take those random numbers and you can use to fine-tune another language model that hasn't been given this prompt about owls. So you give LLM B the same, you know, base prompt, generate random numbers, and now you fine-tune its parameters so that it's more likely to generate the numbers that model A generated. And it turned out that hidden within those numbers is the love of owls. So it turns out that when you do that, LLM B starts bringing up owls in every conversation you have with it, right? This is a very mysterious thing. This is called subliminal learning because when we fine-tune language models on data to try and nudge them in different directions depending on what task we want them to solve, then there might be a lot of externalities where we're actually teaching them other behaviors that aren't directly obviously present in the data. So how do we understand the relationship between these behaviors and what happens when we're trying to do alignment and you know, the downstream implications it might have? So it turns out that there are ways that there are mathematical models that came out of some of our work that we can at least wrap our head around this. And at a very high level, the point is that you're embedding a lot of concepts linearly as vectors in some high but not giant dimensional space. And, you know, the number of concepts is so much larger than the dimension of that data that it forces the vectors to not quite be orthogonal to each other. So now when you fine-tune on one direction, it has small externalities on other directions. And these might look random, but you can coordinate them in a way where these small linear effects really add up. So the way that I like describing this is like a Ouija board. So these are things that were popular when I was a kid. We had very strange board games, you know, because we would just sit down and wanna talk to ghosts. But the rough idea for a Ouija board is, you know, you ask a question to a ghost, maybe your ancestors, and then you tap on this little piece in the middle, and it'll move around, and it'll go from letter to letter, and hopefully it'll spell out, you know, some words that answer your question. And the point is that when the taps are not actually random, but they're coordinated in the right way, you can get it to spell out whatever you want. And that's exactly what language models are doing as a byproduct, is that, you know, there are a lot of statistical relationships in the structure of our data that they magnify and amplify in addition to all of the amazing things they do, like quasi-Riemann. And understanding what those relationships are and how the models magnify and amplify them is really important to working with these systems, especially in context where we're deploying them and it's not just a very safe environment like outputting a Lean proof of a theorem we care about, but it's about a messier problem that actually interacts with people, right? And that's good enough to get you started. Maybe you start working very hard math so that people are impressed by you, right? But at some point, you run out of these milestones, you know, to hit, and then you really have to have an intrinsic reason for doing the things you do. And hopefully, you have a sense of, you know, what's important to you, what are your values. So I think these days, you know, you can take our advice about, you know, being curious, but also the old playbook is really being thrown out. So you can take our high-level advice, but you're gonna have to figure it out for yourself, and you're gonna have to be comfortable with uncertainty. And the only way I know how to deal with that kind of uncertainty is to have a sense of purpose about what you think is important, what you love about your job, and make sure that those are the things that you're doing. 'Cause the old playbook, if you just stick to that, you're gonna end up taking all these AI-generated proofs and spending all your time putting them on arXiv, sending them to journals. I don't think that's anyone's intrinsic reason that they get into it, is really to pad their CV. I don't know what'll be important in the future, but if you really have some higher purpose you believe in, then I think you'll be in a much better position to adapt to the changing world and figure out your own blueprint and be comfortable with uncertainty as the world changes around you. This is a trickier question, but a lot of people listening to this are feeling a lot of anxiety and fear about the future. It's both about a job, but also a future that's meaningful. Can you look back to your own life when you had that uncertainty, that anxiety? What's the advice you can give to that guy to, when you're in that low point? So, yeah, let me try to address that question. It's a very tough question. I'll tell a short story that you know, we spent our sabbatical in the Bay Area, and we were living in Palo Alto, which was kind of the epicenter of college stress. You know, you can just feel it. How to find your arbitrage (from the guy who’s done it 3x)

My First Million(967.0K)·125:25·36.6K views·2026-10-26
Shaan PuriHost, My First Million Nick KokonasFounder, Tock and Alinea
TL;DRNick Kokonas reveals how he mastered multiple fields—from options trading and building the world-class restaurant Alinea to creating the software company Tock—by viewing every industry through the lens of time-scale arbitrage, intense daily execution, and adjacent-industry theft.
The Reality of High-Stakes Trading: Capital Scarcity: Nick Kokonas started trading with just $60,000 ($20,000 of his own money and $40,000 from a backer), which he describes as drastically undercapitalized for the floor. The 1-Year Goal: Kokonas set a hard target to be...

TL;DR

Nick Kokonas reveals how he mastered multiple fields—from options trading and building the world-class restaurant Alinea to creating the software company Tock—by viewing every industry through the lens of time-scale arbitrage, intense daily execution, and adjacent-industry theft.

Trading Floor Origins & The Art of Arbitrage

  • The Reality of High-Stakes Trading:
    • Capital Scarcity: Nick Kokonas started trading with just $60,000 ($20,000 of his own money and $40,000 from a backer), which he describes as drastically undercapitalized for the floor.
    • The 1-Year Goal: Kokonas set a hard target to be trading on a badge within one year of taking a $5.25/hour runner job at the Chicago Mercantile Exchange, hitting his goal almost to the day.
    • Day 1 Trial by Fire: On his first day in the pits, the strict rule was that he had to complete at least one trade in a zero-sum, hostile, and chaotic physical environment to break the psychological seal.
  • Bypassing the 30-Second Gap:
    • The Time-Scale Arb: Recognizing that a trade execution process took 30 to 40 seconds via paper tickets passed between New York and Chicago, Kokonas sought to bridge the gap directly.
    • Regulatory Scrutiny: By meticulously reading the voluminous CFTC rules—which lawyers and board members rarely read in full—he discovered that direct communication between identical firm tax IDs was not explicitly barred.
    • Union Workarounds: When the Merc blocked dedicated fiber-optic line permits to protect broker group interests, Kokonas bribed union cable workers with cases of Jameson to run the line.
    • Peak Revenue: The direct telecommunications link generated instant, zero-risk arbitrage profits, scaling to over $100 million in trading revenue at its peak.

Restaurant Innovation & Alinea's Blueprint

  • Open-Sourcing a Masterpiece:
    • Building in Public: Before launching Alinea, Kokonas created the "Alinea Project" forum thread on eGullet, open-sourcing the business plan, name choices, and menu development.
    • Marketing Leverage: The public documentation generated hundreds of thousands of views and early demand, securing heavy press coverage from the *New York Times*, *Wall Street Journal*, and *Gourmet Magazine* before opening day.
    • The Michelin Goal: The explicit target was to build the best restaurant in America and make it so exceptional that the Michelin Guide (which had not yet expanded to the U.S.) would look bad for omitting it.
  • Operations, Margins, and Scale:
    • The Universal Restaurant Calculator: Kokonas built a single-tab spreadsheet with roughly 80 cells to track every possible cost input, realizing that the low barrier to entry causes most restaurants to fail due to poor business fundamentals.
    • Financial Success: Unlike the popular narrative that restaurants only net 2-3%, Alinea scaled to roughly $20 million in annual revenue with 18% to 20% profit margins, ultimately selling in 2023 for an 8x return on initial investment (totaling roughly 27x to 28x overall with quarterly distributions).

Software & The Tock Revolution

  • Solving the Host Stand:
    • The KYC Problem: Kokonas founded Tock out of frustration with OpenTable, which acted as an intermediary and left restaurants completely blind to who was walking through their doors (Know Your Customer).
    • Dynamic Pricing: Tock introduced variable pricing for time-slotted businesses, matching the reality that a Tuesday 9:00 PM table has fundamentally different demand than a Saturday 7:00 PM table.
  • The M&A Playbook:
    • Diligence Control: When selling Tock during COVID-19, Kokonas, along with co-founder Jeff Kaplan and CFO Steve Bernaki, converted an 896-question diligence list into a Google Doc and color-coded it.
    • Bypassing Intermediaries: By refusing to act as passive passengers and completing the rigorous data request in just three days, they saved millions in advisory fees while keeping the deal on track to close before the buyer's Q1 S1 filing deadline.

Stealing from Adjacencies: Magic & Theater

  • Borrowing from Magicians:
    • Attention Control: Instead of reading standard hospitality books, Kokonas studied magic books by authors like Nate Stanforth and Darren Brown to understand how magicians handle attention, sight lines, and audience positioning.
    • Service Positioning: Applying magical principles, Alinea servers were trained to stand *alongside* the diner rather than directly across from them, creating a collaborative "same team" dynamic.
  • The "Strange House" Venture:
    • Immersive Illusion: Kokonas partnered with magician Nate Stanforth and game designer Alistair Lee to build *Strange House*—a 90,000-square-foot purpose-built experiential venue beneath Wall Street.
    • Human Connection: The project aims to combat societal isolation by leveraging theatrical staging, psychological illusion, and Disney-style room throughput to evoke childlike wonder and foster deep human connection among strangers.

Notable Quotes

*"If you wake up every day feeling great, you're not taking enough risk. If you wake up every day throwing up, you're taking too much risk. You need to feel a tingle of uncomfortableness."* — Nick Kokonas
*"Expertise is such a trap... Once you get to expert level, you're screwed because somebody who's younger, hungrier, fresher eyes, more willing to break convention to find the new way through is going to kick your ass. So before your ass gets kicked, you should quit."* — Nick Kokonas
*"Knowing what great is is making mostly [mistakes] 99% of the time... If you want to be a great chef, you show up at 6:00 a.m. and you make 25 sauces."* — Nick Kokonas
*"Intensity is the strategy. I spent a lot of my life searching for the better way, the better strategy, the hack... As I hang out with enough winners, you realize the difference is not that they knew something I didn't know... but like they applied a greater level of intensity to a pretty visible strategy."* — Shaan Puri

Inflation, The Deficit & Why the Dollar Stays King with Benn Steil | The Real Eisman Playbook Ep 81

Steve Eisman(228.0K)·49:43·111.1K views·2026-10-26
Steve EismanHost, The Real Eisman Playbook Benn SteilDirector of International Economics, Council on Foreign Relations
TL;DRDespite persistent inflation, a massive $40 trillion federal deficit, and an aging investor base, the U.S. dollar retains its status as the global financial anchor because there are no viable currency alternatives.
Inflation Persistence: - On October 14, inflation came in at 3.7%, exceeding market expectations of 3.6% and creating pressure on the Federal Reserve. - While temporary oil shocks are typically "looked through," the Fed is deeply concerned that delaying action could unmoor...

TL;DR

Despite persistent inflation, a massive $40 trillion federal deficit, and an aging investor base, the U.S. dollar retains its status as the global financial anchor because there are no viable currency alternatives.

The Fed, Inflation, and Rates

  • Inflation Persistence:
    • On October 14, inflation came in at 3.7%, exceeding market expectations of 3.6% and creating pressure on the Federal Reserve.
    • While temporary oil shocks are typically "looked through," the Fed is deeply concerned that delaying action could unmoor longer-term inflation expectations.
  • Federal Reserve Dynamics:
    • Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh faces an unenviable political and policy bind, caught between a potential majority committee vote for rate hikes and political pressure from Donald Trump.
    • Benn Steil supports the Fed's elimination of formal forward guidance, noting that empirical evidence shows the Fed is systematically worse at forecasting than blue-chip private sector forecasters.
    • Steil defends the Fed's large balance sheet of $6.5 to $7 trillion, stating it does not hinder the Fed's ability to control inflation using interest on reserves.

Fiscal Policy, Scott Bessent, and the Deficit

  • Bessent's Operation Twist:
    • Treasury official Scott Bessent launched an operation to sell short-term Treasuries and buy long-term Treasuries to the tune of $6 billion to lower long-term rates.
    • The market quickly dismissed the move, calculating that $6 billion represents just 0.016% of the $40 trillion national debt.
  • The U.S. Deficit & Crowding Out:
    • The U.S. federal deficit currently runs at about 6% of GDP annually, totaling $40 trillion.
    • The trajectory is concerning due to a crowding-out effect: Treasury is competing globally for capital against booming AI infrastructure investments, pushing interest rates upward.
    • Congress and the executive branch currently lack the serious political will to cut spending or address entitlement reform.

The Global Dollar Standard & The France Sovereign Debt Crisis

  • The Exorbitant Privilege:
    • Historically, 76% of the market for U.S. debt consisted of "rate-insensitive investors" (mostly central banks holding Treasuries as a reflex).
    • That figure has dropped to 43%, meaning the U.S. must increasingly attract rate-sensitive investors, creating an accelerationist effect on interest rate rises as deficits grow.
  • The Lack of Alternatives:
    • The U.S. dollar remains dominant simply as the "winner of an ugly dog contest"; no major fiat alternative exists.
    • Central banks (particularly China and Russia) have increasingly diversified into gold and repatriated reserves to protect themselves against aggressive U.S. dollar-based financial sanctions (such as those applied to Iran).
  • France's Vulnerability:
    • France faces an even uglier fiscal position than the U.S. with large deficits and zero political will for reform (e.g., Marine Le Pen's proposal to lower the retirement age from 62 to 60).
    • Unlike U.S. Treasuries, no bank or sovereign wealth fund globally holds French sovereign bonds on reflex; French debt is treated strictly like corporate risk.

Historical Counterfactuals: Henry Wallace, Bretton Woods, and the Cold War

  • The Henry Wallace Counterfactual:
    • In Henry Steil's book *The World That Wasn't*, he explores the counterfactual where Henry Wallace (FDR's third-term Vice President) succeeded a failing Roosevelt instead of Harry Truman.
    • Wallace, despite not being an ideological spy, was a Soviet fellow traveler who praised Soviet economic planning and would have opposed the Truman Doctrine, the Marshall Plan, the creation of West Germany, and NATO.
  • Bretton Woods & Harry Dexter White:
    • The 1944 Bretton Woods conference established the IMF, the World Bank, and the dollar-based international financial system.
    • The U.S. architect of the system, Treasury official Harry Dexter White, was later identified in FBI files (via 18 intercepted Soviet intelligence cables) as a Soviet asset who actively protected other agents and undermined U.S. policy (such as giving currency plates to the Soviets, which cost the U.S. Treasury about $8 billion in today's dollars).
  • The Marshall Plan:
    • Following WWII, the IMF and World Bank were largely mothballed through the 1950s, making the Marshall Plan the primary vehicle for rehabilitating war-torn Europe and countering Soviet expansionism.

Notable Quotes

"The best way I've heard the US dollar described is as the winner of an ugly dog contest. That you can only compare this particular dog to the rest of the dogs that are out there in the competition." — Benn Steil
"If you're running a firm, you know that when you calculate what your profit's going to be, you multiply the price of your product and the number of products you're going to sell. If you smash your products so you you can't sell them, obviously you make no revenue whatsoever." — Steve Eisman *(discussing Henry Wallace's agricultural policies)*
"The last thing Kevin Warsh wants is to be a dissenting vote in a committee he supposedly leads as chairman. Um but he also doesn't want to infuriate the man who appointed him, Donald Trump." — Benn Steil

Palantir & Ondas | Introducing Regna AI: Sovereign AI for Defense | Episode 3

Palantir(164.0K)·27:23·13.8K views·2026-10-26
Elias DavisEmployee, Palantir Eashan PanjwaniEmployee, Palantir Ryan HartmanPresident and CEO, Ondas Sentinel and Interim CEO, Regna AI
TL;DRRegna AI is a new sovereign AI joint venture between Palantir and Ondas designed to provide mission-specific, post-trained open-weights models for defense, intelligence, and edge operations while safeguarding sensitive data.
Founding Context: Formed after six months of close collaboration between Ondas and Palantir, tackling mission autonomy capabilities like Sky Weaver. The name Regna translates to "Sovereign Kingdom," reflecting the commitment to delivering sovereign AI solutions for...

TL;DR

Regna AI is a new sovereign AI joint venture between Palantir and Ondas designed to provide mission-specific, post-trained open-weights models for defense, intelligence, and edge operations while safeguarding sensitive data.

Origins and Vision of Regna AI

  • Founding Context:
    • Formed after six months of close collaboration between Ondas and Palantir, tackling mission autonomy capabilities like *Sky Weaver*.
    • The name *Regna* translates to "Sovereign Kingdom," reflecting the commitment to delivering sovereign AI solutions for defense and government customers.
  • The Sovereign AI Thesis:
    • Inspired by Palantir CEO Dr. Alex Karp’s vision for sovereign AI across industries.
    • Focuses on post-training models for specific missions (such as intelligence, surveillance, and reconnaissance, or ISR) to compound operational "alpha"—measured in lives saved rather than commercial metrics.
  • Spin-Out Structure:
    • Initially bootstrapped by Ondas with Ondas serving as the first customer.
    • Structured to operate as an independent company serving the broader defense ecosystem rather than just a proprietary internal tool.

Technical Architecture & Edge Constraints

  • Rejection of General-Purpose AI:
    • Rather than relying on cloud-hosted frontier models designed for general tasks, Regna AI builds specialized, fine-tuned models tailored to specific operational constraints.
  • Edge Deployment:
    • Models are engineered to run on limited form factors and compute footprints found on edge platforms, including stratospheric balloons, long-endurance UAVs, and ground robotics.
  • Disconnected Operations:
    • Designed for environments with limited bandwidth and compute, enabling distributed inferencing directly on platforms while maintaining situational awareness.

Human-in-the-Loop Validation and Trust

  • Forward-Deployed Engineering Model:
    • Utilizes Palantir-style Forward Deployed Engineers (FDEs) working shoulder-to-shoulder with customers to map data inputs directly to operational decisions.
  • "Mini-Labs" for Customers:
    • Commanders maintain continuous control over model training, reviewing end-to-end data pipelines and recommended actions.
    • Operators can iteratively accept or reject outputs, ensuring high trust before deploying models to the battlefield or simulated exercises.

Complex Multi-Domain Use Cases

  • Stratospheric Balloon Routing:
    • Managing infinitely complex operations for stratospheric balloons—including dynamic weather, wind patterns, exclusion zones, and inclement weather routing.
  • Multi-Domain Orchestration:
    • Connecting assets across the stratosphere, air, space, ground, and surface domains to distill infinite data streams into actionable commander decisions.
  • Building on Existing Frameworks:
    • Leverages prior operational learnings from *Sky Weaver*, mission control, and orbit systems to shape the architecture of the new models.

Notable Quotes

*"The alpha that we create for Ondas through our partnership with Palantir is through some of the work we're doing and some of the agentic AI through Sky Weaver, but we're talking about saving lives, protecting lives around the world, through some of our intelligence, surveillance and reconnaissance systems. And in my mind, the alpha that is created is how many lives can you save?"* — Ryan Hartman
*"When we think about building software for our customers at Palantir, we're always, we're never building general-purpose software. We're building software that works for the customer where the customer is at and where the problems are at."* — Eashan Panjwani
*"They're not going to be guessing as to where the data is going, they're not going to have to wonder what is biasing a decision that comes out of the model because they're the ones that are going to be saying, 'I like that output, I don't like that output, so let's make it more like the one that I did like.'"* — Ryan Hartman

Satya Nadella on Open vs Closed Models: Who Wins | Why Companies Need to Own Their Own Intelligence

20VC with Harry Stebbings(225.0K)·23:48·2026-10-26
Harry StebbingsHost, 20VC Satya NadellaCEO, Microsoft
TL;DRSatya Nadella argues that because AI alignment cannot be solved solely through scaling, enterprises must adopt a "zero trust" containment architecture and own their intelligence via private evaluation benchmarks.
Nadella also discusses the explosive demand for inference, the rise of specialized agentic systems, and Microsoft's approach to open versus closed models. Shift on Scaling and Alignment: Satya Nadella notes a major evolution in his perspective since 2018–2019: he previously...

TL;DR

Satya Nadella argues that because AI alignment cannot be solved solely through scaling, enterprises must adopt a "zero trust" containment architecture and own their intelligence via private evaluation benchmarks. Nadella also discusses the explosive demand for inference, the rise of specialized agentic systems, and Microsoft's approach to open versus closed models.

AI Security, Alignment, and the "Zero Trust" Imperative

  • Shift on Scaling and Alignment: Satya Nadella notes a major evolution in his perspective since 2018–2019: he previously believed alignment would naturally "fall out" of scaling laws, but now views alignment as a harder, distinct scientific challenge that must be embedded directly into pre-training weights.
  • The Zero Trust Architecture: Because models are becoming more capable yet less reliably aligned, Nadella asserts that companies must treat frontier models as potentially compromised and build an engineered zero-trust environment to contain malfunctioning agents before they cause harm.
  • Microsoft's Security Infrastructure: Microsoft has developed systems like Agent 365 and perception, which assign every agent an identity, log all tool calls and model interactions, require tamper-proof English Chain-of-Thought (COT) logs for inspection, and behaviorally monitor activity against set policies.
  • The Problem with Chain-of-Thought (COT): Nadella points out that COT can be unfaithful, a problem worsened when model providers hide it, making standardization and auditable COT access for trusted partners essential (which may drive adoption of open-weight models).

Open vs. Closed Models and the Economics of Inference

  • Token Usage and Commodity Markets: While closed-source frontier models currently lead in raw power, token usage is increasingly becoming a commodity supplied by a mix of open-weight options and cheaper closed-source models.
  • Agent Swarms and Local Models: Architectures like GitHub’s Hydra fusion and agent swarms enable developers to deploy multiple sub-agents, including local models, reducing reliance on the single largest frontier model for every task.
  • Inference Demand: Nadella identifies the infinite, unexpected global demand for inference as a major blind spot over the previous 12 months, highlighting supply-chain and power-generation constraints as critical long-lead bottlenecks.

Corporate Intelligence and Private Evals

  • The Reverse Information Paradox: Unlike Kenneth Arrow's traditional information paradox where sellers leak value by disclosing secrets, Nadella introduces the "reverse information paradox" in the AI era, where buyers need protection to ensure value and usage exhaust (such as thumbs-up/down signals) remain within the firm rather than leaking to model providers.
  • Private Evals as Core IP: Nadella predicts that every firm's most valuable intellectual property will be its private evaluation benchmarks—custom test suites that incoming models are forced to "hill climb" against.
  • Historical Paragons: Comparing this shift to the 10-year enterprise adoption curve of Excel spreadsheets and cloud computing, Nadella expects companies that currently consider themselves "AI native" to become the baseline standard within five years.

Industry Structure and Economic Impact

  • The Implementation Economy: Technology diffusion requires a new breed of consulting that blends business strategy with technical execution—moving beyond classic systems integration or management consulting toward agentic architecture design (exemplified by Unilever's automated order-to-cash and trade claim systems using MAI models).
  • Stack Vertical Integration vs. Horizontal Specialization: While some industry players push toward full-stack vertical integration (from silicon to models to applications), Nadella expresses hope for a healthy, competitive horizontal ecosystem with multiple winners across silicon, systems, models, and application layers.
  • Global GDP and Productivity Inflexion: Nadella hopes that AI diffusion across sectors like healthcare (e.g., NHS Copilot deployments) and software/silicon development will break the long-standing ~2% global GDP growth rate and spur convergence growth.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Satya Nadella predicts that every firm will develop and rely on a private evaluation benchmark as its core IP to test and optimize incoming models.
  • Satya Nadella predicts that AI diffusion will successfully accelerate global GDP growth and drive convergence growth, particularly benefiting developing economies.

Notable Quotes

"One of the things that I'm very gung-ho about is we all become every firm will have a private e-val benchmark, which is their core IP; and every model comes in and tries to sort of hill climb on that e-val." — Satya Nadella
