The $2B AI Startup Simulating Human Behavior with 85% Accuracy

Sourcery with Molly O'Shea(77.3K)·56:15·6.0K views·2026-10-26
Molly O'SheaHost, Sourcery Joon Sung ParkCo-Founder and CEO, Simile
TL;DRSimile is a $2B AI startup building foundation models of human behavior through digital twins, backed by $300M in total funding from Greenoaks, Index Ventures, Fei-Fei Li, and Andrej Karpathy.
By combining large language models with agentic architectures and partnerships with over 40 million real people globally, the company’s technology has demonstrated 85%+ accuracy in predicting human behavior and is currently used by enterprises like CVS, Wealthfront, Deloitte,...

TL;DR

Simile is a $2B AI startup building foundation models of human behavior through digital twins, backed by $300M in total funding from Greenoaks, Index Ventures, Fei-Fei Li, and Andrej Karpathy. By combining large language models with agentic architectures and partnerships with over 40 million real people globally, the company’s technology has demonstrated 85%+ accuracy in predicting human behavior and is currently used by enterprises like CVS, Wealthfront, Deloitte, and Banco Itaú to simulate decisions before real-world deployment.

Origins & Research Evolution: From "Smallville" to Foundation Models

  • Academic Origins: The company evolved from *Smallville*, a 2023 research experiment led at Stanford by Joon Sung Park and co-founders Michael Bernstein and Percy Liang.
  • The Smallville Experiment:
    • A game town (resembling Pokemon or Sims) populated by 25 autonomous AI agents powered by GPT-3/3.5 paired with an agentic architecture featuring memory, planning, and reflection.
    • Agents performed daily routines, organized a Valentine's Day party, and formed relationships with zero pre-programmed scripts, demonstrating emergent behavior.
  • Core Breakthrough: Proved that foundational LLMs could be prompted to generate novel, human-like behaviors and interact within simulated communities.

Technology, Methodology & Validation

  • Individual and Environmental Modeling: Simula models individuals first (learning base traits like risk tolerance and aesthetics) and then places them into simulated environments to observe chaotic, emergent societal interactions.
  • The "Gap" in Human Behavior:
    • Surveys show normal distributions (what people *say* they will do, e.g., going to the gym).
    • Actual behavior follows an exponential curve driven by activation energy and "optionality" (opportunity costs and available alternatives).
  • Validation & Accuracy:
    • Early testing relied on human evaluators assessing agent believability ("illusion of life").
    • Scaled to a representative sample of 1,000 U.S. participants using voice-to-voice AI interviews to build digital twins, predicting actual behavior with 85%+ accuracy after a two-week blind test.
  • Data Quality Controls: Partnered with Gallup (holding a panel of hundreds of thousands built over decades) and implemented attention checks (arithmetic, recall) and sparse polling intervals to prevent respondent fatigue and gaming.
  • Informed Consent: Excludes underage populations and unconsenting individuals; otherwise aims for a complete representative snapshot of society, capturing both positive and negative human tendencies.

Business Model, Products & Enterprise Use Cases

  • Platform Offering: Delivered via a SaaS web interface where enterprise users define target populations and ask unlimited questions to receive instant simulations with an accompanying confidence score (0-100 range probability, akin to a scientific p-value).
  • Vertical-Specific Agent Banks: Maintains pre-built simulated panels across key verticals, supplemented by custom data collection reaching tens to hundreds of thousands of real people weekly (with a 24-hour turnaround for new panels).
  • Key Enterprise Customers:
    • Retailers: CVS (optimizing store layout, messaging, and decor).
    • Financial Institutions: Wealthfront, Deloitte, and Banco Itaú (Brazil) for testing policies and communicating corporate values.
    • Other Verticals: Entertainment, CPG, and automotive companies.
  • High-Stakes Use Cases: Concept testing, conjoint analysis, max-diff analysis, messaging optimization for electric vehicles, internal HR retention modeling, and recruitment strategy.

Team Composition & Company Operations

  • Team Scale: Approximately 100 employees split across science/modeling (20–25%), engineering/product (including talent from DoorDash), and a heavily for-deployed go-to-market motion led by co-founder Laney Yellen.
  • Academic Roots: Roughly 20% of the team consists of labmates and academic relatives from Stanford's computer science department.
  • Investor Base: Backed by Greenoaks, Index Ventures, Fei-Fei Li, Andrej Karpathy, and Percy Liang, alongside angel investors drawn to the inevitability of social simulation.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Industry Maturity Timeline: Joon Sung Park predicts that the simulation industry will reach the maturity level where the coding agent industry is today within 1.5 to 2 years.

Notable Quotes

"The best way to understand something is to recreate it... Simulation is a lot like creating a portrait of who we are as a society." — Joon Sung Park
"People are actually not that interested in predicting the future... if I told you that your market is going to crash in two quarters, they're all going to then ask, 'What do we need to do now to change that?' But it's always that second part that matters more: so what do we need to do now to shape the future?" — Joon Sung Park
"These are people whose greatest prize in winning the current game is gaining the right an invitation to play the next game." — Joon Sung Park

So that's ultimately what building simulation here at the societal scale really is going to look like. And so this all started with Smallville. It did. Okay, explain what that is and like your whole background behind that. For sure. So, Smallville was an experiment that we ran back in 2023 and 2022 where we had the initial observation that these large language models—initially, we were using GPT-3 and then GPT-3.5. The models were still fairly janky, but you could see glimpses of opportunity where these models were trained on such broad human data that when you poke at the right angle, you can actually extract a lot of human data that appeared in its training data. This might be from the web, Wikipedia, some of the social media. And what we really started to ask ourselves is, can we actually use this to create an interaction paradigm that is truly new? That goes beyond just simply generating context or classifying different tasks, but can we actually generate truly new behaviors? And we decided that the most ambitious version of this is actually to create a simulation of an entire person. And then once we had an entire person simulated, can we actually go create an entire community? So Smallville is an experiment that came out of that effort. So it was a game town. Computer scientists love games because it's an amazing way to demonstrate new capabilities. So we created a game town that looks a lot like what Pokemon, Sims, like these kinds of towns might look like. And then we populated this game town with 25 agents. And these agents all had different names, different characteristics, different life stories. And they were basically language models paired with what we called back then an agentic architecture that had memory, planning, and reflection. So they would actually wake up in the morning, do their routines, go to work, have relationships. The simulation itself actually takes place the day before Valentine's Day. So these agents will actually come together to have a party. So there is a character that's actually organizing a Valentine's Day party. There are characters that are asking themselves out. And none of this was pre-programmed. But what we could see is that we can basically create simulations of these people with emerging phenomena just coming out of the simulation. So that was the experiment that we ran in 2023 that was Smallville. That particular work ended up introducing a few key concepts. One was that we can create simulations of people, and the other was the core agentic architecture and that we can create agents using generative AI models. It was the very early days of these agentic workflows, so we could really start to see them becoming possible. And then we've been building on the technology basically for us to then create simulations that are more and more accurate, and that are more diverse, and that are bigger in scale. How do you test the validity of this? Like how do you know if it's actually working? So our ability to test actually has shifted quite a bit also alongside technological progress. On day one when we created Smallville, what we actually did was we interviewed them. We interviewed the agents. What we've done is we pulled individual agents out of this game town, sat them in this hypothetical chair on a command line, and we would basically ask them questions, and these questions would probe certain qualities of these agents that are similar to how people would reason about different tasks in their lives. So simple question: tell me your name. And then we would ask: tell us your life story. We would then ask questions like: who's the one person you want to spend time with? And then we would ask: if you had to get that person a present, what would you get them? We would ask these questions because these are questions that when you think about the human brain and human mind, we can only answer these questions because we can remember, because we can plan ahead, but also because we can reflect. Then what we ended up doing was we generated all these responses, and then we actually had human evaluators come in and take a look at these responses and said, given the experience that the agents had in their game town experience, are these experiences or insights actually humanlike? So we actually basically have human evaluators compare between human authors' generations and these agents' responses to see which one's more realistic. And we've actually done quite well, that these agents were actually quite realistic. And the way to sometimes tell is imagine you're asking this agent, who do you want to hang out with the most? It's a little bit strange if the agent says whoever they happen to see the most on the street. The agent should likely say the person they actually care about. Who do you have a special relationship with? What kind of conversations did you have with people? What actually stood out? Then that agent should really reflect on that and pick those people. So those are some of the experiments that we ran, and those are the signals that we were able to get. But then over time we wanted to go beyond that. So here really the initial evaluation really was about believability, that we start to see what we call the illusion of life in these agents. But as we matured as a field, what we started to do was to run evaluation that is now about more of a faithfulness and accuracy. So the experiment that we ended up running following that was we ran an experiment where we then took this architecture to create simulations of a thousand real people. So that started with us actually going out recruiting a large number of real people that sample representatively from the US. So it's a thousand representative sample of the US population across gender, race, ethnicity, and so forth. And then we actually collect the data from these people. So we would actually do this time human interview. So we actually built an AI agent that can talk voice-to-voice with people. Have those agents talk to people voice-to-voice. We would ask questions that are a little bit like the questions we ask agents in Smallville. Tell me the story of your life. Where did you grow up? What were some of the hard decisions you had to make? Who matters to you? We'll use that data to then train AI agents that can basically be the twins of these people. And then we would send people away for two weeks, bring them back to a lab, and then this time we would actually ask people to complete a battery of surveys, behavioral studies, respond to stimuli, and have their agents predict what they would say. And at the end of 2024, first for the first time really in the field, we could actually show that we can predict people's behavior with 85% plus accuracy with these agents. That's ultimately what gave the company foundation for today's technology. Of course, the technology itself has shifted quite a bit. But that was the initial building block that we could see that these models and these agents can now actually predict human behavior, and this is an amazing building block. But at the same time, it's a large data operation, that we do not simply create simulations out of a vacuum. We believe strongly that the simulation has to be grounded and it has to be evaluated alongside people. So it really is both. I have so many questions. Do you sometimes inject like anomalies or things that could create some factors that are not predictable? And then my second question, which I wanted to be my first question, is human behavior predictable? In the training data, we actually do inject a lot of behavioral knowledge. It's not just about what they say, but it's also really about what they do. And there's a lot of irrational behaviors in human tendencies. So a lot of things that we study in economic behavior games, randomized control trials, they all try to cover those. At the same time, there are certain things in people that are really not known to change. Your risk tolerance, as I briefly mentioned, it's not really known to change in a person. So once we are able to learn that, then this actually becomes a trait that becomes very predictable. So it really is about understanding the gap between what people think they are and what they actually do in their life. And really the modeling challenge here actually is really learning that translational layer. How do you take observed behaviors or attitudes into their fundamental tendencies? And what is in that gap? Is it time? Is it events around you? Like how do you determine that gap that ultimately creates the outcome? So the part that is actually very consistent in our experimentation is these two functions are actually governed by different functions or different sort of a shape of the curve. What people say in a survey actually often just follow a normal distribution, right? So when I say, you know, am I likely to go to the gym this week? I likely say with some normal distribution curve, you know, yes, maybe, you know, seven-point scale, I likely say five to six every week. Now, do I actually go to the gym that week? The activation energy for me to actually go to the gym is actually way higher than what I would expect. So that looks much more like an exponential curve where it has a much clearer drop-off in terms of what kind of activation we need to actually get to that state of, oh, I'm actually going to exercise this week. So those are the kinds of different functions that we need to learn, and that's where all of the gap is. Again, have so many questions about this. What is the number one or the most common indicator of human behavior? Like what are those traits or things that you've noticed create the reactions or events? What causes people to do things? Why would someone make a decision versus not? Like what are those? How do you determine if someone will continue a pattern or not? So, turns out what this actually has to do sometimes actually is the existence of optionality. So let's actually briefly take a look at how psychologists would usually study human behavior in a survey. We have an abstract question and we have basically a certain set of options that were presented in multiple-choice questions or on a Likert scale of one to seven. That's a very confined space. But when people behave, they don't usually behave in a vacuum or in abstract conditions. They often behave in the real world, right? And what that basically means is even simple questions like, "Where do we want to go eat?" In abstract, if somebody said, "Hey, let's go eat X," the odds are if I'm really hungry—in fact, if I'm very hungry and if there's no food available—I almost certainly say, "Yes, let's go do that." But what often ends up happening is we do not have access to certain options, but we have access to other options. This is where people have this optionality. So now they have to think, they have to think about the opportunity cost of taking that option. So that's actually what really governs people's behavior, that, yes, I can go exercise this week, but if I don't, I can sleep for one more hour or I can take one more meeting. It's these kind of things. Optionality and the type of optionality often really governs people's behaviors. Do you pull a lot of data from dating apps? Not dating apps in particular. No, we don't. Okay. So, taking all of this into consideration, how does this then become the business of Simile? So Simile is a company that is creating a foundation model of human behavior that basically can take as input a description of a person or a group of people and then simulate what these people might do in different contexts. So today, Simile's use cases would actually go into things like surveys, experiments. Many of the insights teams have a lot of studies they were meant to run. Now what Simile can do is actually run them, but in a much faster pace, in a much, with much less resources. So that's obvious use cases. So we do concept testing, and in more complex cases we do conjoint analysis, max-diff analysis, the traditional analysis that researchers had to run today that would have taken them sometimes hundreds of thousands of dollars, sometimes millions of dollars over many years. We can run them in simulation basically almost instantly. They are just a query away. So that's the use cases today. But at the same time, the users then come to us and ask, okay, it's great that we can now do these studies. Can we then do something that our studies couldn't do? What would people actually do? How would they actually react to different stimuli? So this is where simulation really is about also creating a model of the environment, not just the people. So imagine that we have customers—just a dumb example here. Imagine you're trying to sell water. It's actually quite interesting. If this as a behavior question is actually quite interesting. Imagine there's only one source of water, then that particular person can likely charge us a million dollars to just have access to water. But the reason why that cannot happen is because of the optionality, because there's a context around the water source where it is everywhere, right? It's about packaging, it's about of course the right stream, but at the end of the day this is widely available, that there are other optionalities in this space. So we want to actually be, want to, we want to model that. We want to model that environment. What are the other options? What are the other alternatives? Given that as the context, can we then expose our agents to this decision-making? At what price would you actually buy this water given all this environmental factors that goes into this? So, it really is about modeling people and their behavior, but it's also about modeling the environment and everything that surrounds them. And who are some of your customers? What types of companies are these? Yeah. So we work across a few different verticals today. Right now, one of the larger verticals is retailers. Okay. But one of the customers that we've been working with is, of course, CVS, and they have worked with us to create simulations of both how to better understand their consumers so that they can better serve them both in terms of the store layout, the messaging, and the overall decor. But we also work with financial companies, so think some of the largest financial institutions today. But we also work with international institutions like Banco in Brazil, where they also have similar needs, but they want to understand, well, what kind of policies or what kind of messaging do we need to put out to better communicate our values to our customers? And of course we also work with entertainment companies, CPG companies. So the verticals are pretty broad, but what unites them is their need that they need to better understand their user base, that they need to be and they're trying to be even more customer-centric, and today there's a real limitation to be able to talk to every single person every time they need to talk to them. But if we can create simulations of people and be able to ask any questions at any time, that becomes a superpower for these companies. For each one of these customers, do you create their own digital twins? Like how do you create net new populations within that instead of using the same base for everything where you get duplicates? Right. So Simile is maintaining vertical-specific agent banks across the key verticals. So these are actually simulated panels that are always available on day one. So when we get into a new customer relationship, that's always available. Now then our customers often have further asks, right, because no one's actually interested in general population; everyone's interested in their own unique population that they're trying to better understand. So in those contexts, Simile would actually go collect more data to create and train the model to be able to cover those populations. And today, Simile has partnerships with over 40 million real people across the panel partnerships that we have. This is across the globe, and at any given moment we're talking to tens of thousands, if not hundreds of thousands of real people every single week. So that's the scale of operation that we are running today. So if there's a new need for new panel members, that can usually be met within 24 hours. Who are these people? I'm always really curious about this 'cause I don't understand polling. I don't understand when people say it's 53% of Americans think X or Y. It's like, who are these Americans? Who? I don't know. I've never met someone who's been polled before or surveyed. Have you? Well, you have. So, but yeah, like who are these people? Yeah. So the way this operates, so these are people like just everyday people who answer to—who chooses obviously to answer to polling or decide to participate in these data collection processes. The magic to this process often is, however, knowing how to sample them. So social scientists have been developing methods for randomized samples, probability sampling, to really get a broad understanding of the representative set of people. In some cases, some of these panel companies, they specialize in this exercise, and what that means for them is they actually try to form a real relationship with people across the entire country. One of the strategic partnerships that Simile has actually is with Gallup. Gallup, of course, one of the most—I would consider Gallup to be one of the most gold standard polling companies or companies that are generating these kind of insights. They have a Gallup panel of hundreds of thousands of people with whom they've actually built up relationships over the past decade, two decades, and so forth. But wouldn't those people be biased at some point or feel entitled to, like, give them what they think would be like the best information based on their own circumstances or something? Like how do you actually know that that's valid information? Yeah. So there we control for these a couple of ways we control. One obviously is—and the biases actually can crop up in different ways. So there are different ways to fight for different kinds of biases, and you know, not surprisingly, this entire thing is an area of study, like it's an entire academic field with many researchers working on this. But for instance, one of the clear biases that we often see is fatigue. If you get asked the same questions, or even different questions, but if you get polled every single week, they start to really get fatigued, and they start to answer in ways, or they try to game the system, right? Oh, I want to—if I participate in this, I get paid more. So I just give some answer and I just participate. You start to see those behaviors. So what you do is you actually time it so that the participation is far and few in between and sparse enough while being able to still maintain that relationship. And we would actually run studies that basically show we have a new population versus a population who had answered X number of questions over the past. What is the right amount of distance to have between asking different questions to get the high quality, what we would consider to be the gold standard quality responses? We would actually measure that. Another thing that we would do is often times in these kind of studies we also do attention checks. So these are basically questions that are dummy questions that are not actually a part of the core measurement. Yeah. Oh, you're basically sneaking in questions that would filter out participants who are not actually paying attention. What would one of those questions be? Question might be something like, imagine right after our interview you asked me what was the first conversation—what was the first question that you asked me in today's conversation? If I can't answer, that's tricky. You asked me about Elon, are we living in a simulation? Question. I think you—I should know that if I was actually paying attention to our conversation. But that's an example of attention check. I was thinking like something really silly. Checking for someone's attention. Like how tall is a giraffe? How many hamburgers can a human eat? So technically that's actually also a good attention check. Really? Yeah. They You can use those if you want. Thank you. This truly—I mean, but these are actually questions that we would sometimes ask, too. Some surveys or data collection processes would literally ask people to do very simple arithmetic so that, like, you know, if it's—if they say 10 plus 10 is 30, then we know they're likely not paying attention, or they're not, or they're—or they might not be good at math in those instances. That brings up another question. I don't know how to—I'm just—I'm now in the mood. So for us, the no-gos are actually participants who cannot give informed consent. So we take informed consent very seriously, that every member of the panel who joins our study and our data collection process, they know why the data is getting collected, at least broadly, and they consent to that process, and they also have the opportunity to withdraw their consent. And this would also generalize to other populations that cannot give informed consent. For us, we are trying to create models that can actually model the diverse viewpoints, the values, preferences, and tastes of people. A part of it is also then learning the good behaviors, but also bad behaviors of people. Simile needs to be able to capture those. So like what is the process at which this outputs this information? What our users have today is they have access to an online—it's a—we basically serve a SaaS platform to our users. So they get access to this web interface where they can basically come in and define two things. They can say who they want to talk to and what questions they want to ask. The core function attached to these reports in relation to what I'm just saying is this idea of "motor of confidence" or "predicted accuracy." So this actually motor is actually quite important to us. So Simile will be generating predictions, simulations of future alongside the core foundation model that we are training to make the simulation, though we are jointly training a predictive model of accuracy where its job is one thing. Its job is to output a zero-to-100-range probability on how confident we are in this particular response on the basis of all the data that we used to train our model and the question that we got. And this is sort of like in inferential statistics, if you're a social scientist, it's a little bit like having a p-value. And in science, when a p-value is less than 0.05, that's sort of your canonical way of saying we're ready to make a decision. We provide that. So if confidence is higher than 90%, or usually 85%, our customers have found it to be robust evidence that can actually be used to make very high-stakes decisions. So it is actually the whole package. So it is about delivering the insight and then also delivering evidence that is undeniable, that, well, if this is the evidence, then this is a totally reasonable outcome that we have to prepare for, and then providing this model that basically is giving us a degree of confidence calibration. It's really interesting. One of the questions I had was, what is the most expensive mistake Simile helped a customer avoid? Yeah. What is it? So there's actually an interesting study that we actually ran. This technically was slightly be—this was before Simile, but I still mention it because I think as an example I think this is quite interesting. Jeez. These are the findings that are not just narrowly focused on one particular question, but it's more generalizable, but you're looking at the entire field holistically. Simulations allow you to explore that, and when it's at its best, it actually should surface insights that are counterintuitive, that are surprising. There are so many actors with competing incentives who have their own things they're trying to optimize for. So if we can simulate that over and over again and see what kind of outcome we might get depending on what policy, that could be potentially quite powerful. It's like transaction data, mobility data. Makes a lot of sense. But what really matters is actually the causal mechanism. It's the randomized control trials. Imagine you have the same experimental setup and you're varying a particular variable, whether it's a product feature, whether it's messaging, and actually observing people's behavior change. And that data actually matters a tremendous amount, because what we've learned over time is people are actually not that interested in predicting the future—which to me that was a little bit surprising—because unless you're trying to win against the stock market, what are you going to do if I told you that your market is going to crash in two quarters? They're all going to then ask, "Wow, that's shitty. What do we need to do now to change that?" But it's always that second part that matters more: so what do we need to do now to shape the future? And this is where for us it's not just about finding correlation. It's about understanding the causal mechanism, the causation, because that's ultimately the lever that we have to shift the direction of the future. So who makes up the team? The New Full-Stack Designer

Y Combinator(2.4M)·65:44·43.1K views·2026-10-26
Marvin SchwaiboldLead, Product Design Studio, Shopify Aaron EpsteinHost, Y Combinator Design Review
TL;DRMarvin Schwaibold of Shopify and Aaron Epstein of Y Combinator review how AI tooling is shifting product design toward a full-stack, editor-driven workflow, and evaluate motion and interactivity across five viewer-submitted startup websites.
Shift to AI-Driven Workflows: Marvin Schwaibold explains that while design processes remain iterative, tooling has moved from static aesthetic layouts in Figma to terminal-based AI agents (like Codex and Cloud Code) that rapidly produce real, testable demos. The Designer as...

TL;DR

Marvin Schwaibold of Shopify and Aaron Epstein of Y Combinator review how AI tooling is shifting product design toward a full-stack, editor-driven workflow, and evaluate motion and interactivity across five viewer-submitted startup websites.

Shopify's AI Product Studio & Workflow Evolution

  • Shift to AI-Driven Workflows: Marvin Schwaibold explains that while design processes remain iterative, tooling has moved from static aesthetic layouts in Figma to terminal-based AI agents (like Codex and Cloud Code) that rapidly produce real, testable demos.
  • The Designer as Editor: Marvin Schwaibold notes that AI handles the heavy lifting of code and execution complexity, placing humans in the driving seat to curate, quality-control, and steer outputs based on what looks and feels right.
  • Shopify's Dual-Track Initiative: Marvin Schwaibold describes his team's work over the past year touching core surfaces, split into two areas:
    • Admin Experience: Redesigning and polishing interfaces for web and the mobile app (the "green app").
    • Sidekick: Building AI infrastructure that allows merchants to manage and automate their stores conversationally via chat.
  • Prototyping for Buy-In: Marvin Schwaibold emphasizes that tangible prototypes built with AI spread through an organization like wildfire and secure founder/executive alignment much faster than traditional PRD documents.

Independent Studio Roots & The "Primal" System

  • Molly Studio Background: Marvin Schwaibold highlights his time running the Brooklyn design studio Molly alongside partner J Tal, working with major clients like Collins, Apple, and Google with a tight team of two to three concurrent clients.
  • The Primal Content System: Marvin Schwaibold explains this structured methodology used to digest heavy client information into exploratory website flows where content is progressively disclosed rather than demanding attention.
  • Collins Bookshelf Craft: Marvin Schwaibold details a hand-crafted bookshelf component built with engineer Jesper featuring organic, level-up bounce animations designed as an "Easter egg" to signal agency-level dedication and craft to potential clients.
  • Joining Shopify: Marvin Schwaibold shares that Tobi Lutke’s mandate—stating that the future needs to be designed and requiring designers to paint a picture—inspired him to bring his studio into Shopify for massive-scale impact.

Grass Shader & DialKit Prototyping

  • Weekend Grass Shader Project: Marvin Schwaibold details a personal project (available on GitHub via MarvinSchwaibold/grass-shader) converting real grass video footage into a web shader with 217,000 individual grass blade variations.
  • Iterative AI Prompting: Marvin Schwaibold describes feeding video, screenshots, and topographical height maps into ChatGPT and Codex agents over multiple prompts to correct underlying terrain geometry and lighting.
  • DialKit Integration: Marvin Schwaibold explains how Josh introduced DialKit (run via `npm run` from the website) to add binary toggles and variable sliders, exposing underlying structures to instantly spot errors and tweak values like grass length and wind movement.
  • Carousel Iteration: Marvin Schwaibold shares that a portfolio carousel inspired by Mike Matas took 91 prompts to get right, utilizing a side parameter panel to dial in physical properties like "pull boost."

Viewer Website Reviews (Motion & Interactivity)

  • Nstack: Marvin Schwaibold and Aaron Epstein praise the retro, whimsical desktop-emulator feel and dropping users straight into the product, but criticize visual overload, dense sidebars, and competing CTA buttons that violate the rule of clear visual hierarchy.
  • SkillSync: Marvin Schwaibold and Aaron Epstein note that while dark mode and dark aesthetic are clean, the page is a "mile wide and an inch deep," burying key information behind unclicked sidebar tabs and utilizing superfluous animations that do not aid user comprehension.
  • Instaplay: Aaron Epstein and Marvin Schwaibold highlight the instant engagement of interactive prompt-to-game capabilities, but critique the immediate paywall/sign-up friction and hidden navigation paths that obscure the primary goal of creating games.
  • Netlify (Mentlify): Marvin Schwaibold and Aaron Epstein praise the clean, trustworthy presentation, reductive typography, strong social proof (Microsoft, Anthropic, Coinbase), and breathable, well-spaced chapter-based scroll flow.
  • Nullify: Aaron Epstein and Marvin Schwaibold commend the high-quality instructional videos, but criticize the competing headline placeholders, difficult click targets on carousels, and the use of sluggish scroll-jacking that hinders native user scroll speed.
  • Instinct: Aaron Epstein and Marvin Schwaibold praise the immersive 3D/video elements and brave serif font choices for space tech, but recommend instantly triggering hover states and adopting Blossom Carousel (from the open-source Blossom Carousel GitHub repo) for smoother trackpad scrolling.

Notable Quotes

"We're more the editors now than ever before... AI is definitely raised the ceiling for all of us, but it's not a crafter. It can execute complexity... but it doesn't know what looks good and what looks bad and that's for me to decide." — *Marvin Schwaibold*
"Smooth scrolling on websites is a disaster in the industry... You want interruptible software because our thought patterns are interruptible... You would never want to get a user into an environment where they have to smooth scroll or scroll jack through the content." — *Marvin Schwaibold*
"Codex won't get mad at me if I need 91 approaches at getting the animation correct. If I had done that with my engineer, they would probably flip out." — *Marvin Schwaibold*

US Treasury Secretary Bessent to Iran International: Iran’s economy to shrink by over 25%

Iran International(1.2M)·16:45·2026-10-26
Scott BessentUS Treasury Secretary Kambiz TavanaHost, Iran International
TL;DRUS Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent detailed the ongoing "Operation Economic Outcast" against Iran, predicting a 25%+ contraction in Iran's economy by March 2027 and advising Iranian citizens to hoard fuel and withhold taxes.
Bessent stated that the US naval blockade is airtight, regional financial nodes are being systematically severed, and the Trump administration remains resolute regardless of US midterm elections. - Operation Outcast Progress: Launched roughly 8 weeks ago as an escalation of the...

TL;DR

US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent detailed the ongoing "Operation Economic Outcast" against Iran, predicting a 25%+ contraction in Iran's economy by March 2027 and advising Iranian citizens to hoard fuel and withhold taxes. Bessent stated that the US naval blockade is airtight, regional financial nodes are being systematically severed, and the Trump administration remains resolute regardless of US midterm elections.

Campaign Status & Economic Impact

  • Operation Outcast Progress: Launched roughly 8 weeks ago as an escalation of the maximum pressure campaign initiated in March 2025.
  • Inflation & Currency: Inflation has surpassed 100%, and the regime has reportedly stopped publishing the true value of the rial, which Bessent estimates is trading around 3 million to the dollar.
  • Global Compliance:
    • Several Gulf countries have severed financial ties.
    • Mahan Air has halted all flights except to Russia and China; other airlines defying restrictions face imminent shutdown by Treasury.
  • Humanitarian Channels:
    • Treasury maintains open humanitarian channels for food and medicine, but Bessent noted the regime is too broke to pay for international imports due to lack of dollars.
    • The central bank continues printing money, driving inflation while failing to pay government salaries.

Cracking Down on International Financial Networks

  • Targeting Trustees & Brokers:
    • Treasury is targeting trade and financial facilitators operating from Dubai to Hong Kong.
    • Many trustees are refusing to return funds to Tehran, viewing the regime as a bankrupt entity.
  • International Banking Interventions:
    • Treasury has subpoenaed a Greek bank, sent information requests to six European banks, and targeted institutions across Pakistan, Dubai, Egypt, Turkey, and Russia.
    • Specific actions include shutting Dubai branches of an Egyptian bank, closing a Turkish bank, and targeting VTB (the second-largest Russian bank) for aiding the regime.
  • Cryptocurrency & Asset Tracking:
    • The regime's use of digital currencies is viewed as an act of desperation.
    • Treasury is tracking and escrowing illicit crypto and luxury assets (such as expensive apartments) to recover funds intended for the Iranian people.
  • Whistleblower Rewards:
    • Treasury offers a whistleblower program where citizens, diaspora members, and embassy employees can claim up to 30% of recovered regime funds by reporting illicit channels via the Treasury website.
    • Sanitization and un-sanctioning are offered to brokers who come forward and cooperate.

Naval Blockade & Energy Sector Collapse

  • Strait of Hormuz Control:
    • The US Navy controls the strait, refuting regime claims.
    • Energy is flowing out nightly at rates exceeding pre-war levels.
  • Oil & Gasoline Shortages:
    • Iran has effectively "no oil on the water" and zero loadings at Kharg Island.
    • Domestic gasoline production is faltering in a country with the world's third-largest energy assets.
  • Military Neutralization:
    • Iran's navy and air force have been neutralized.
    • Substantial missile and drone stockpiles were destroyed, alongside 85% of Iranian missile factories.

Political Isolation & Regime Dynamics

  • Internal Fractures:
    • Economic pressure is causing visible infighting among politicians, the IRGC, and the Supreme Leader.
    • Public figures (such as President, Speaker Ghalibaf, and the head of the central bank) have issued economic warnings over the summer, followed by some IRGC members.
  • Leadership Isolation:
    • Bessent characterized IRGC commanders as "rats in a sewer pipe" hiding in deep locations and remaining blind to the suffering of ordinary Iranians.
  • Midterm Election Calculus:
    • Bessent dismissed regime hopes that upcoming US midterm elections would shift Washington's calculus, stating President Trump's resolute blockade and policies remain entirely unchanged.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Economic Contraction: Scott Bessent predicts that Iran's economy will contract by more than 25% between March 2026 and March 2027 — a contraction exceeding that of the Soviet Union after its collapse.
  • Campaign Intensity: Scott Bessent claims that the next eight weeks of Operation Economic Outcast will become "even more intense."
  • Advice to Citizens: Scott Bessent advises Iranian citizens to stop paying taxes and immediately stock up on heating fuel and gasoline ahead of an impending winter shortage.

Notable Quotes

"I would predict that for from March of 26 until March of 27 the economy would contract more than 25% which is more than the Soviet Union contracted after uh its collapse." — Secretary Scott Bessent
"My advice to the Iranian people would be don't pay your taxes because you're not going to get anything back from the government. They're going to keep cutting social benefits." — Secretary Scott Bessent
"There has never been anything uh this powerful before. The kinetic wartime action... The blockade is the airtight, watertight. Nothing is coming in or out." — Secretary Scott Bessent

So, a couple of things there. So many of the people who are holding money outside of Iran do not want to send it back in. But for those who do want to report these people who are engaging in illicit activities outside or inside the country, show us the channels. Treasury now has a reward system where whistleblower Iranian citizens can make up to 30% of what we recover, and we want the money for the Iranian people. So it's on the Treasury website for our whistleblower program, and we've had numerous people contact us already. So if you are an Iranian in Iran, if you are part of the diaspora, if you are an employee in a consulate or embassy around the world, contact us and we are happy to pay the reward. Mr. Secretary, last week addressing these trustees, you said either you're with us or against us. So these brokers, what does the against part look like? Is there an offramp for these people to leave the regime? Of course. If they come to us—we've sanctioned many of them, we sanctioned one very important individual last week—if they want to come to us and cooperate, again, they could claim 30% of what they're holding for the regime. We are happy to unsanction individuals who work with us because, again, our goal is to bring the regime to the negotiating table. They've been murderous since last December when the protests started. So whether it is crypto, whether it is expensive apartments in Iran, we are tracking the money and we are escrowing that money. You recently said Treasury mapped every node, every facilitator, and every network. Now we are seeing some of the IRGC members start to warn about the economic consequences. But part of my worry is many of the IRGC commanders, they are like rats in a sewer pipe. They are in hiding, in deep dark locations, and they don't see what's happening to the Iranian people. The Ayatollah is very isolated. So many of these leaders do not understand what is happening to the Iranian people, but the frontline people, the politicians, and many of the IRGC members who are out in public can see things falling apart. Washington Today (10/26/26): Trump and Vance Campaign in Republican States as 2026 Midterms Near

C-SPAN(2.0M)·51:36·2.8K views·2026-10-26

TL;DRAs the 2026 midterm elections approach, President Trump and Vice President Vance are campaigning across traditionally Republican states, while competitive Senate, House, and gubernatorial races nationwide see millions in spending and intensifying legal and political battles.
Executive Branch Campaign Blitz: President Donald Trump: Traveling to Corinth, Mississippi for a rally supporting Senator Cindy Hyde-Smith against Democrat Scott Colom. Held a weekend rally in Port Huron, Michigan alongside Republican Senate candidate Mike Rogers. Vice...

TL;DR

As the 2026 midterm elections approach, President Trump and Vice President Vance are campaigning across traditionally Republican states, while competitive Senate, House, and gubernatorial races nationwide see millions in spending and intensifying legal and political battles.

Executive Briefing & Campaign Trail Updates

  • Executive Branch Campaign Blitz:
      • President Donald Trump: Traveling to Corinth, Mississippi for a rally supporting Senator Cindy Hyde-Smith against Democrat Scott Colom. Held a weekend rally in Port Huron, Michigan alongside Republican Senate candidate Mike Rogers.
      • Vice President JD Vance: Campaigned in Belgrade, Montana, backing Republican Aaron Flint in the competitive 1st Congressional District race against Democrat Sam Forsdag ($4.3M raised vs. Flint's $2.8M; both rated tossups by Cook Political Report and Inside Elections). Vance also criticized Montana's Democratic Senate contenders and headed to Kansas for an evening event.
  • Key State-Level Senate and Gubernatorial Races:
      • Texas (Senate & Governor): Democratic Senate candidate James Talarico and Republican Ken Paxton are locked in a statistical tie in a race exceeding $100M in spending. Rice University Professor Mark Jones noted Elon Musk is spending ~$200M independently via the Republican Senate SuperPAC. Governor Greg Abbott is running for re-election, facing Democrat Gina Hinojosa.
      • Ohio (Governor): The *Cleveland Plain Dealer* reversed its policy to endorse Democrat Amy Acton over Republican Vivek Ramaswamy, citing a campaign ad involving Acton's childhood sexual abuse and Ramaswamy's refusal to answer questions or debate. Ramaswamy defended the ad on Fox News.
      • Michigan (Senate): Democratic Senate candidate Abdel El-Sayed campaigned in Detroit, criticizing Mike Rogers' congressional record on the national deficit and corporate consolidation, while Rogers criticized El-Sayed's background at Trump's Port Huron rally.
      • Iowa (Senate): Republican Representative Ashley Hinson and Democrat Josh Turek are in a tight race. Hinson addressed attack ads regarding stock sales and her husband's business, while Turek emphasized his bipartisan appeal from a working-class background.

Legal Actions, Justice Department, and White House Policies

  • Alaska Senate Election Lawsuit:
      • DOJ Action: The Justice Department filed a lawsuit to stop Alaska from automatically certifying Senate election results if incumbent Republican Senator Dan Sullivan loses, citing voter confusion due to a retired school teacher named Daniel J. Sullivan also being on the ballot alongside Democrat Mary Peltola.
      • Reactions: The Peltola campaign and allied groups asked a federal judge to fast-track a decision. Attorney General Todd Blanche defended the DOJ's move on CNN, while Senator Lisa Murkowski and Senator Adam Schiff criticized the lawsuit as federal overreach and an attempt to subvert the election. Peltola's campaign also reported she suffered minor injuries in a weekend car crash.
  • Maureen Comey Reinstatement: U.S. District Judge Jesse Furman ruled that former Assistant U.S. Attorney Maureen Comey was "unlawfully terminated" in July 2025 in violation of separation of powers and ordered her reinstated.
  • Press Freedom Lawsuit: Press freedom groups, backed by Columbia and Yale legal clinics, sued the Trump administration over bans preventing CNN, MS Now, and Politico from the White House, arguing it violates citizens' First Amendment rights to government information.

Foreign Policy and National Security

  • Russia-Ukraine War & Starlink:
      • Trilateral Talks: House Speaker Mike Johnson confirmed the White House is in discussions regarding trilateral talks to end the war, following a recent hostage release involving Stephen Hubbard.
      • SpaceX/Starlink: Elon Musk confirmed SpaceX restricted Ukraine's use of Starlink due to terms of service violations regarding offensive military operations, though Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky confirmed the service was fully restored. Colorado Democrat Jason Crow criticized the unilateral control held by a private tech figure over national security operations on CBS.
  • Cabinet Accountability: Kentucky Republican Thomas Massie posted about potentially reintroducing articles of impeachment against Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, while House Democratic Leader Hakeem Jeffries stated House Democrats intend to hold Hegseth accountable.

International Elections: Israel's Knesset Vote

  • The Electoral Landscape: Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu faces a tight electoral test on Tuesday against former IDF Chief of Staff Gadi Eisenkot, with 120 seats in the Knesset requiring a 61-seat coalition. Turnout is projected high (75-80%), which could squeeze out smaller parties.
  • Key Issues: The legacy of October 7th looms large, including demands for an independent National Commission of Inquiry regarding government accountability and security failures.

Notable Quotes

"We're not going to send them to Washington D.C., we're unfortunately going to keep them right here in Montana, where they can't do as much bad." — Vice President JD Vance
"It cannot and should not be the case that a tech oligarch, a private citizen who has that much control over our national security, who is unaccountable, who is unelected with zero transparency is making life and death decisions on the battlefield..." — Representative Jason Crow
"We are running against the most corrupt politician in Texas. Ken Paxton represents everything that's wrong with our politics. He doesn't serve us. He serves himself." — James Talarico
"There are moments when a community must decide what it stands for, when its institutions have a duty to speak, and when silence itself becomes a statement. Ohio has reached such a moment." — The Cleveland Plain Dealer Editorial Board

From NBCNews.com: After three years of navigating war, crisis, and global outcry, Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu is facing one of the most significant electoral tests of his decades-long political career. As Israelis go to the polls on Tuesday, Netanyahu's party is neck-and-neck with former Israel Defense Forces Chief of Staff Gadi Eisenkot, who has emerged as his strongest rival in a campaign that has revolved around everything from Israel's security and military conscription to bread-and-butter issues like the economy. Jerusalem Journal author Avi Mayer talked to us yesterday about how Israeli elections work. What happens is Israelis across the country will vote for one of several dozen parties, and they will then have seats assigned to them in the Knesset, which is Israel's parliament, which has 120 seats. In order to have a commanding majority, an individual who is contending to be Prime Minister would have to assemble 61 of those 120 seats. That is an extraordinarily difficult thing to do. No one party has ever been able to achieve that majority on its own, and so you have to assemble a coalition in order to do that. So what we're in for is several weeks and perhaps months of give-and-take negotiations on both sides of this debate: Who is able to form a governing coalition? And there's a non-zero chance that neither contender, neither Benjamin Netanyahu nor Gadi Eisenkot, will be able to do so, in which case we will go for a second round of elections sometime in early 2027. What is turnout like in these elections, and how do you think this election in particular will compare with others in the past? So, election turnout in Israel is generally fairly high, well over 70%, and the guess at this point is that it's going to be significantly higher even than that, perhaps 75 or even 80. that is a major factor here, because what it means is that some of the smaller parties may not make it into the Knesset. The allocation of seats is based on your ability to garner at least 3.25% of the overall vote, and if that number is higher, then some of the smaller parties may not make it in, which would strengthen these larger parties who are contending for the premiership. and so it'll be very interesting to see what the voter turnout is, both amongst Jewish Israelis and the Arab community, which make up about 20% of Israel's voters, and how that impacts the overall total. Now this is the first of the election since the attacks of October 7th. Avi Mayer talked about how that day is affecting this year's campaign. Well, October 7th looms large in the Israeli consciousness. We are still in living trauma of that horrific day, the memory of 1,200 people who were massacred, 251 hostages, many of whom did not return home alive, and so one of the main questions in Israel is one of accountability. Now one of the charges made by the opponents of Benjamin Netanyahu is that he has not taken responsibility for the failures of that day or of what led to that day, and one of the sort of symbols of that has been the refusal on the part of the current government to establish the National Commission of Inquiry to get to the bottom of how October 7th happened and ensure it never happens again. Now, the coalition will say that they are in favor of some kind of commission, but it has to be government-appointed, and that they have to be able to determine who's a part of it in order to ensure that it is not, ingrained with some kind of anti-government bias. Of course, his opponents will say, "Well, that would not be a fair co- commission, it has to be one that is totally independent, one that can really investigate, get to the bottom of what happened, and perhaps assign personal responsibility to those individuals who are currently in government." And so, that has been a major bone of contention, in this election. It's been one of the main campaign promises, frankly, of the change block who've said that on Day One, they will establish this Commission of Inquiry in order to get to the bottom of what happened on October 7th, and ensure it never happens again, but that is just one way in which October 7th plays very prominently in this election campaign. Why OpenAI Is Racing to Make Computer Use Superhuman

a16z(305.0K)·44:14·14.8K views·2026-10-26
Ari WeinsteinEmployee, OpenAI James SunEmployee, OpenAI Seema AmbleHost, a16z
TL;DROpenAI's computer-using agents, supercharged by the Astra model, are now faster than average humans at operating software and entering a massive scaling phase across consumer and enterprise markets.
Led by OpenAI's Ari Weinstein and James Sun, the company is tackling enterprise adoption through deterministic sandboxes and human-in-the-loop controls, while driving consumer adoption through proactive "dots" and universal web navigation. Speed and Efficiency: Faster...

TL;DR

OpenAI's computer-using agents, supercharged by the Astra model, are now faster than average humans at operating software and entering a massive scaling phase across consumer and enterprise markets. Led by OpenAI's Ari Weinstein and James Sun, the company is tackling enterprise adoption through deterministic sandboxes and human-in-the-loop controls, while driving consumer adoption through proactive "dots" and universal web navigation.

Astra Model Capabilities & Performance

  • Speed and Efficiency:
    • Faster Execution: Ari Weinstein noted that Astra is faster on average than previous models because it uses fewer thinking tokens to accomplish the same tasks.
    • Approaching Superhuman: The models are now faster than the average human at using a computer and are getting extremely close to being faster than expert human users.
    • Video Game Benchmarks: Internally, Astra plays video games against older models like `5.6` and wins every time, successfully beating games like *Fallout* in roughly 30 hours.
  • Reliability and Creative Work:
    • Edge Reliability: Reliability has increased in hard-to-perceive ways that matter significantly in practice, moving from roughly 97% to 99% reliability.
    • Creative Tasks: Excels at creative work, such as drawing things in Microsoft Paint during live voice interactions.
  • Alignment and Security:
    • Enhanced Safety: James Sun highlighted that Astra is OpenAI's most aligned model, featuring system cards showing much better resistance to prompt injections and unintended actions.
    • Enterprise Trust: This alignment makes it significantly safer for sensitive enterprise use cases like procurement and vendor/customer communications.

Enterprise Automation & Legacy Software

  • Boring Opportunities in Systems of Record:
    • Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP): Automates complex legacy software that lacks APIs and has been used for 15 to 20 years.
    • Procurement Workflows: Replaces giant spreadsheets (e.g., ordering lab equipment) and manual ops data-entry with automated end-to-end purchasing and vendor outreach.
    • Finance Reconciliation: Operates financial software and multiple ledgers at month-end to reconcile expenses without needing custom APIs.
    • Sales & CRM: Gathers prospecting data and inputs records across disparate sales software and CRM systems.
  • Vertical Industry Adoption:
    • Restaurant Group Example: Software uses back-storage photos and vision to project traffic, automate grocery ordering via platforms like Shopee, and adjust employee timetables in ERP systems based on historical data.

Enterprise Controls & Guardrails

  • Deterministic Sandboxes:
    • Application and URL Restrictions: Enterprise admins can create a sandbox around the agent to deterministically restrict which apps, websites, and data sources it can access.
  • Human-in-the-Loop Dials:
    • Auto-Approve System ("Guardian"): A smart model evaluates every agent action to decide if it is safe and aligned.
    • Custom Intervention Levels: Enterprises can dial in specific requirements—such as mandating human approval for file downloads, uploads, or visits to new websites.
  • Design & Trust Building:
    • Visual Cues: Features a visible cursor (affectionately called the "Codex cursor" by the team) and fun animations so users can watch the agent perform tasks cleanly and transparently.

Consumer Use Cases, "Dots," & Proactivity

  • Automating Drudgery:
    • Daily Tasks: Fills out DMV forms, unsubscribes from email lists, organizes personal finance spreadsheets, and manages meal planning.
  • Cloud Computers and "Dots":
    • Dedicated Cloud Environments: Users can spin up "dots," each getting its own cloud computer and full web browser to execute tasks.
    • Proactive Interventions: James Sun shared how dots proactively catch missed calendar invites from external Gmail emails and monitor Slack channels to surface critical feedback and parse Sentry logs.
  • Consumer Growth & Form Factors:
    • YC-Level Growth: Following the integration of browser use into existing products, OpenAI has seen YC-level week-over-week growth.
    • Mobile Expansion: The next inflection point involves bringing powerful cloud computing and agentic workflows to mobile devices for billions of everyday users.

The Future of Software: Legacy vs. AI-Native

  • Coexistence of Legacy and New Software:
    • Prolonging Legacy Systems: Because computer use interacts with software the same way a human does (via UI rather than APIs), it extends the utility of 10-to-40-year-old legacy software.
    • New Software Standards: Standards like WebMCP are being adopted in in-app browsers, allowing agents and humans to share the interface collaboratively (e.g., agents doing tedious work while humans handle final transactions).
  • Hyper-Personalized Software:
    • Micro-Applications: The low cost and complexity of creating software means future applications can be custom-tailored for 100 people or even a single individual (such as custom tactical trainers built for family members).
    • Shared Intelligence Layers: The vision points toward coherent user experiences where applications, browsers, and cloud agents share context rather than operating as isolated silos.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Software Interoperability: "All the software is going to work with agents by default and I think webmcp is a great example of this and I think we'll see more stuff like that." — Ari Weinstein
  • UI Evolution: "I expect both of those things to continue for a long time... I don't think that that stuff's [legacy software] going to go away overnight." — Ari Weinstein
  • The Scaling Moment: "I think we're at the scaling moment of computer use both for consumer and enterprise. Yeah. At the same time." — James Sun

Notable Quotes

"Computer use is not like a thing you pick up and just say I want to do something with it. It's really like the universal kind of API that the model has to do whatever it takes." — James Sun
"Humans are so hardwired for direct manipulation. You really want to be able to, you know, move and and and do things with your own hands." — Ari Weinstein
